# Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback

Daniil Tiapkin Affiliation: CMAP, CNRS, École Polytechnique, IPP
Affiliation: LMO, Université Paris-Saclay
Email: [daniil.tiapkin@polytechnique.edu](mailto:)    Daniele

Affiliation: Duisburg-Essen University Affiliation: HSE University
Email: [denis.belomestny@uni-due.de](mailto:)    Éric Moulines
Affiliation: Mohamed Bin Zayed University of AI Affiliation: LRE EPITA
Email: [eric.moulines@mbzuai.ac.ae](mailto:)    Alexey Naumov
Affiliation: HSE University Email: [anaumov@hse.ru](mailto:)    Kashif

###### Abstract

Traditional Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) often
relies on reward models, frequently assuming preference structures like
the Bradley–Terry model, which may not accurately capture the
complexities of real human preferences (e.g., intransitivity). Nash
Learning from Human Feedback (NLHF) offers a more direct alternative by
framing the problem as finding a Nash equilibrium of a game defined by
these preferences. While many works study the Nash learning problem
directly in the policy space, we instead consider it under a more
realistic policy parametrization setting. We first analyze a simple
self-play policy gradient method, which is equivalent to Online IPO. We
establish high-probability last-iterate convergence guarantees for this
method, but our analysis also reveals a possible stability limitation of
the underlying dynamics. Motivated by this, we embed the self-play
updates into a proximal point framework, yielding a stabilized
algorithm. For this combined method, we prove high-probability
last-iterate convergence and discuss its more practical version, which
we call Nash Prox. Finally, we apply this method to post-training of
large language models and validate its empirical performance.

## 1 Introduction

Aligning powerful pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) with complex
and often subjective human preferences and values is a central challenge
for safe and beneficial AI. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback
(RLHF) ([Christiano et al., 2017](#bib.bib39)) addresses this by
learning from human preference signals rather than sparse or
hand-engineered reward functions. RLHF has been successfully used to
fine-tune LLMs for tasks such as summarization ([Stiennon et al.,
2020](#bib.bib20)), dialogue, and question answering ([Ziegler et al.,
2019](#bib.bib19); [Ouyang et al., 2022](#bib.bib27); [Bai et al.,
2022](#bib.bib18)).

A common approach within RLHF, rooted in contextual dueling
bandits ([Yue et al., 2012](#bib.bib15); [Zoghi et al.,
2014](#bib.bib10); [Bengs et al., 2021](#bib.bib13)), is to posit an
underlying reward model. The most prevalent choice is the Bradley–Terry
(BT) model ([Zermelo, 1929](#bib.bib12); [Bradley and Terry,
1952](#bib.bib26)), which assigns each action a scalar reward and models
pairwise preferences as a function of reward differences. Under BT, the
goal reduces to learning the reward and choosing an action that is
preferred, on average, to alternatives. In Social Choice Theory, this
corresponds to a Condorcet winner.

However, reward-model approaches can be misspecified ([Dudík et al.,
2015](#bib.bib24); [Munos et al., 2023](#bib.bib25)). In particular, BT
imposes transitivity: if $`a`$ is preferred to $`b`$ and $`b`$ to $`c`$,
then $`a`$ must be preferred to $`c`$. Real human judgments often
violate this property, exhibiting intransitivity ([Gardner,
1970](#bib.bib11); [Tversky, 1969](#bib.bib17); [Klimenko,
2015](#bib.bib5)). Moreover, even when individual preferences are
transitive, group aggregation can create cycles ([May, 1954](#bib.bib9);
[Kreweras, 1965](#bib.bib16)). Such non-transitive preferences can
preclude a Condorcet winner and, more broadly, a consistent scalar
reward function matching all comparisons.

Nash Learning from Human Feedback (NLHF). To avoid assuming a consistent
reward or a Condorcet winner, [Dudík et al. (2015)](#bib.bib24) proposed
a preference-based approach for dueling bandits, later termed Nash
Learning from Human Feedback (NLHF) by [Munos et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib25). NLHF models pairwise preferences via a symmetric
two-player game in which each player proposes an action. The objective
is to find a symmetric Nash equilibrium (NE, [von Neumann
1928](#bib.bib6); [Nash Jr 1950](#bib.bib7)), called a von Neumann
winner (VNW) in the dueling bandits literature ([Dudík et al.,
2015](#bib.bib24)). Unlike a Condorcet winner, a VNW is a mixed policy
and remains well-defined under intransitive preferences.

Regularization and the parameterized setting. In LLM post-training, we
want to align to preferences while staying close to a fixed reference
policy (for example, an instruction-following model). We therefore
consider the NE of a _regularized_ preference game, adding a penalty
proportional to the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence from the current
policy to the reference policy. While many works analyze algorithms for
regularized NLHF by exploiting convexity in the policy space ([Sokota et
al., 2023](#bib.bib38); [Munos et al., 2023](#bib.bib25); [Cen et al.,
2024](#bib.bib14)), less is known when the policy is _parameterized_ (as
in neural networks) and trained via stochastic gradient methods.

Our approach. We close this gap by analyzing the simplest self-play
policy gradient (SPG) under parameterization. Under a leave-one-out
advantage estimator, SPG iterations coincide with Online
IPO ([Calandriello et al., 2024](#bib.bib28)). We prove high-probability
last-iterate convergence and identify a stability condition on
regularization; beyond it, the dynamics may be unstable.

Motivated by this observation, we wrap self-play updates in an (inexact)
proximal-point outer loop ([Martinet, 1970](#bib.bib3)). The resulting
method uses two anchors: the reference policy (to limit drift from the
base model) and the previous outer iterate (to stabilize dynamics). This
yields high-probability last-iterate convergence without a stability
restriction on the regularization parameter. We also propose a practical
variant, Nash Prox, which approximates the proximal anchor via an
exponential moving average (EMA), and show competitive performance on
synthetic preference games and LLM post-training.

Contributions. We summarize our contributions as follows.

- •
  We analyze SPG (equivalently, Online IPO under leave-one-out advantage
  estimator) for regularized NLHF under general policy parameterization,
  which includes softmax parametrization, proving high-probability
  last-iterate convergence under a stability condition on the
  regularized game.
- •
  We embed SPG into an inexact proximal-point framework (PP-SPG),
  achieving high-probability last-iterate convergence in the
  parameterized setting without extra stability conditions.
- •
  We develop a practical deep-learning implementation of PP-SPG called
  Nash Prox, based on an EMA target anchor, and show competitive results
  on synthetic games and LLM post-training.

## 2 Related work

Nash Learning from Human Feedback. The NLHF framework was introduced by
[Munos et al. (2023)](#bib.bib25), building on the formulation of
contextual dueling bandits as a symmetric two-player game by [Dudík et
al. (2015)](#bib.bib24). They proposed the $`\mathtt{NashMD}`$ algorithm
and showed that it enjoys last-iterate convergence to the VNW of a
regularized preference game at a polynomial rate.

Subsequent work studied how to approximate a VNW in the _unregularized_
preference game, e.g., via regret-minimization tools ([Swamy et al.,
2024](#bib.bib8); [Wu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib36)), optimistic mirror
descent variants ([Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib34); [Wu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib53)), and meta-algorithms that ensure asymptotic
convergence ([Liu et al., 2024b](#bib.bib52)). A separate line of work
improved guarantees in the regularized setting ([Zhang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib35); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib33); [Tang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib79)), but these methods typically operate in the policy
space via direct distributional updates. A notable exception is the work
by [Zhou et al. (2025)](#bib.bib31), which provides a policy-gradient
interpretation but requires samples from a uniform distribution over the
action space, which is impractical in the LLM setting where actions are
sentences.

Separately, [Calandriello et al. (2024)](#bib.bib28) showed that the
online version of IPO ([Gheshlaghi Azar et al., 2024](#bib.bib1))
converges to the VNW, but without explicit rates. In this work, we give
high-probability last-iterate convergence guarantees for Online IPO in
the parameterized setting. The result follows from its equivalence to
SPG under a leave-one-out advantage estimator and holds under a
condition on the regularization coefficient. We also introduce a
stabilized proximal-point variant that uses Online IPO as a building
block.

Most of the above work focuses on single-step preference feedback.
Addressing multi-turn decision making, [Shani et al. (2025)](#bib.bib46)
studied preference feedback in settings requiring planning, proposes a
self-play mirror descent based algorithm, and proves convergence to a
Nash equilibrium. Another line of work extends the NLHF to a Stackelberg
principal-agent formulation, breaking the symmetry between max and
min-players ([Choi et al., 2025](#bib.bib80); [Pásztor et al.,
2025](#bib.bib81)).

Policy gradient methods. A large body of work studies policy gradient
(PG) methods for unregularized reinforcement learning with general
parameterized policy classes. A widely used set of sufficient conditions
combines regularity of the score function with Fisher non-degeneracy and
(approximate) compatibility ([Sutton et al., 1999](#bib.bib67)),
enabling global convergence and finite-sample guarantees beyond the
tabular setting ([Papini et al., 2018](#bib.bib65); [Huang et al.,
2020](#bib.bib66); [Liu et al., 2020](#bib.bib64); [Agarwal et al.,
2021](#bib.bib68); [Yuan et al., 2022](#bib.bib57); [Ding et al.,
2022](#bib.bib62); [Fatkhullin et al., 2023](#bib.bib63); [Lu et al.,
2024](#bib.bib30)). These analyses typically consider a _fixed_
objective and quantify progress using smoothness and gradient-dominance
conditions on the induced objective in parameter space.

Regularized policy gradients. Less is known about _Kullback-Leibler
(KL)- or entropy-regularized_ objectives under parameterization, despite
their prominence in RLHF and preference optimization. For
entropy-regularized MDPs, [Mei et al. (2020)](#bib.bib29) proved global
convergence of softmax PG and showed gradient dominance is non-uniform
without control of the minimum action probability. Subsequent work
analyzes regularized gradient flows and stability ([Leahy et al.,
2022](#bib.bib77)), and uses projection/truncation to maintain a minimum
action probability ([Zhang et al., 2021](#bib.bib54); [Labbi et al.,
2025](#bib.bib70)); see also [Liu et al. (2024a)](#bib.bib78) for
convergence over a wider range of step sizes. For two-player zero-sum
Markov games, [Zeng et al. (2022)](#bib.bib72) studied regularized
gradient descent–ascent with softmax policies under partially decoupled
updates (alternating steps and unequal step sizes). In contrast, our
self-play policy gradient uses a single timescale with simultaneous
updates and a shared learning rate, so the opponent evolves at the same
rate each step, making the analysis more challenging.

## 3 Setting

We consider a contextual dueling bandit setting
$`(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{P})`$, where $`\mathcal{X}`$ is a
context space, $`\mathcal{Y}`$ is a finite action space, and
$`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\in[0,1]`$ is the probability that
action $`y\in\mathcal{Y}`$ is preferred to $`y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}`$
given context $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, which satisfies the symmetry
condition:
$`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)=1-\mathcal{P}(y^{\prime}\succ y\mid x)`$
for all $`x,y,y^{\prime}`$. A policy
$`\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$ maps contexts to
probability distributions over actions, where $`\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$
is the probability simplex over $`\mathcal{Y}`$. Let $`\Pi`$ be the
space of all such policies. For a context $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, we define
the expected preference of a policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$ over $`\pi^{\prime}`$,
as:

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime}|x)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi(\cdot|x),y^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}(\cdot|x)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}|x)}}\right]\,.
``` |  |

For a context distribution $`\rho\in\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}`$ we define the
expected preference and Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence as
$`\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime}|x)]`$
and
$`\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\KL(\pi(x)\|\pi^{\prime}(x)]`$.

Regularized preference game. For a fixed reference policy
$`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\Pi`$ we define a *$`\beta`$-regularized
preference* as
$`\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})-\beta\KL(\pi\|\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})+\beta\KL(\pi^{\prime}\|\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})`$,
where $`\beta>0`$ is a regularization parameter. The
$`\beta`$-regularized preference function induces a
$`\beta`$-regularized preference game ([Munos et al.,
2023](#bib.bib25)), which is symmetric, so it admits a symmetric Nash
Equilibrium (NE) $`(\pi^{\star}_{\beta},\pi^{\star}_{\beta})`$ ([von
Neumann, 1928](#bib.bib6); [Nash Jr, 1950](#bib.bib7)). We call a policy
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ a $`\beta`$-regularized von Neumann winner (VNW,
[Dudík et al. 2015](#bib.bib24)) and in particular it satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\in\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})\,.
``` |  |

The $`\beta`$-regularized suboptimality (also known as exploitability
gap) of $`\pi`$ against $`\pi^{\prime}`$ is
$`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\triangleq\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})`$.
The worst-case $`\beta`$-regularized suboptimality of $`\pi`$ is:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\subopt_{\beta}(\pi)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}-\min_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi}\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\pi\succ\pi^{\prime})\,.
``` |  | (1) |

A policy $`\pi`$ is an $`\varepsilon`$-VNW in the $`\beta`$-regularized
game if $`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi)\leq\varepsilon`$. Our goal is to learn an
$`\varepsilon`$-VNW given (i) sampling access to the context
distribution $`\rho`$, (ii) the ability to sample from
context-conditioned policies, and (iii) a stochastic estimate of
$`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)`$ (e.g., from pairwise
comparisons). We measure efficiency via the iteration complexity
$`N_{\mathrm{iter}}(\varepsilon)`$, i.e., the number of algorithmic
updates, and the sample complexity $`N_{\mathrm{sample}}(\varepsilon)`$,
i.e., the number of queries to a comparison oracle.

Value and best-response. Due to the symmetry of the game, it is enough
to consider only the point of view of the min-player, the value of its
policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$ against a competitor policy $`\mu\in\Pi`$ is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\beta}(\pi;\mu)\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\mu\succ\pi)+\beta\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\,.
``` |  | (2) |

Thus, we can define a best-response policy
$`\nu^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu)\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}V_{\beta}(\pi;\mu)`$ and
its value as $`V^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu)`$. We can rewrite the worst-case
suboptimality in terms of value as
$`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi)=V_{\beta}(\pi;\pi)-V^{\star}_{\beta}(\pi)`$.

Additional notations. For a vector $`x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ we define a
span seminorm of $`x`$ as
$`\lVert x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}=\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\lVert x+c{\bf 1}\rVert_{\infty}`$,
where $`{\bf 1}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}`$ is a
vector of all ones. For $`M>0`$ define
$`\mathrm{clip}_{[-M,M]}(x)=\max\{-M,\min\{x,M\}\}`$. For a distribution
$`\rho\in\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}`$ and two functions
$`f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}`$, define
$`\lVert f\rVert_{1,\rho}^{2}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\lVert f(x)\rVert_{1}^{2}]`$
and
$`\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\lVert f(x)\rVert^{2}_{\mathrm{sp}}]`$ .

## 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients

In this section, we consider a simple algorithm for finding a VNW of a
$`\beta`$-regularized preference game with a general parameterized
policy class and a finite action space. We call this algorithm the
Self-Play Policy Gradient (SPG) method.

For a parameter $`\theta\in\Theta=\mathbb{R}^{d}`$, we define
$`\theta\mapsto\pi_{\theta}\in\Pi`$ as a differentiable policy
parameterization. Define a parametrized value as
$`J_{\beta}(\theta;\mu)\triangleq V_{\beta}(\pi_{\theta};\mu)`$ where
$`\mu`$ is a competitor policy. Given this definition and an initial
parameter $`\theta_{0}\in\Theta`$, we define the iterates of SPG as
follows

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta_{t+1}=\mathcal{T}(\theta_{t}-\gamma_{t}g_{t}),\qquad\pi_{t+1}=\pi_{\theta_{t+1}}\,,
``` |  | (3) |

where $`g_{t}`$ is a stochastic gradient estimator of
$`\nabla J_{\beta}(\theta_{t};\pi_{t})`$, and $`\mathcal{T}`$ is an
optional projection-like policy improvement operator (e.g., clipping).
As a main example, we consider the following mini-batch pairwise
REINFORCE gradient estimator ([Williams, 1992](#bib.bib21)), defined as
$`g_{t}=(1/B_{t})\sum_{j=1}^{B_{t}}G_{j}(\theta_{t})`$ for

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
G_{j}(\theta)\triangleq(\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(y_{j}|x_{j})-\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(y_{j}^{\prime}|x_{j}))\cdot\mathrm{clip}_{[-M,M]}(A_{j})\,,
``` |  | (4) |

where $`M`$ is a clipping threshold and $`A_{j}`$ is an advantage
estimate defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle A_{j}\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}-p_{j}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y|x)}-\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y^{\prime}|x)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y^{\prime}|x)}}}\right)\,,
``` |  | (5) |

and where $`x_{j}\sim\rho`$, $`y_{j},y^{\prime}_{j}\sim\pi_{t}(x_{j})`$
and $`p_{j}`$ is a sample from a Bernoulli distribution of parameter
$`\mathcal{P}(y_{j}\succ y^{\prime}_{j}|x_{j})`$. This gradient
estimator can be considered as a leave-one-out (LOO) advantage estimator
([Kool et al., 2019](#bib.bib74); [Ahmadian et al., 2024](#bib.bib73))
with a group of size 2, which was also applied to estimate the
KL-divergence.

Connection with Online IPO. Recall the Online IPO loss

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{IPO}}(\theta)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,y,y^{\prime}\sim\mathtt{sg}(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|x)),p\sim\Ber(\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}|x))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\bigg(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}{\pi_{\theta}(y^{\prime}|x)}\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y^{\prime}|x)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y|x)}\bigg)-\frac{p}{2\beta}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\,,
``` |  | (6) |

where $`\mathtt{sg}`$ as a stop-gradient operation. It is known
([Calandriello et al., 2024](#bib.bib28), Proposition 4.2) that expected
gradients of Online IPO loss are the same of the self-play updates.
However, it is easy to check the sample-based version
of ([6](#S4.E6 "In 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
have exactly the same (stochastic) gradients as a pairwise REINFORCE
estimator ([4](#S4.E4 "In 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
used in our analysis, up to a constant scaling and clipping. In
particular, it allows us to extend all our theoretical results to a
parametrized version of online IPO, and also directly interpret Online
IPO as a LOO-variance-reduced self-play policy gradient update with a
group size of 2.

### 4.1 Theoretical Guarantees

We emphasize that the primary challenge in the parametrized setting lies
in the intrinsic non-convexity of the value $`J_{\beta}(\theta;\mu)`$
with respect to its first argument. Consequently, in the absence of
additional structural assumptions, global convergence of the method
cannot be guaranteed.

###### Assumption 1 (Parametrization regularity, informal).

A pair $`(\theta\mapsto\pi_{\theta},\mathcal{T})`$ satisfies the
following properties: (A1) Lipschitz parametrisation
$`\|\pi_{\theta}-\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{1,\rho}\leq G\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}`$;
(A2) $`L`$-smoothness of $`\theta\mapsto J_{\beta}(\theta;\mu)`$; (A3)
(approx.) Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PL) inequality:
$`\|\nabla J_{\beta}(\theta;\pi_{\theta})\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon_{\rm PL}\geq 2m\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{\theta})`$
for $`\theta`$ in the range of $`\mathcal{T}`$; (A4) $`\mathcal{T}`$
non-increase suboptimality; (A5) $`g_{t}`$ has a bias
$`\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}`$ and is subgaussian with a
variance-proxy $`\propto M^{2}/B_{t}`$, where $`M`$ is a clipping
threshold and $`B_{t}`$ is a batch size.

These conditions hold for context-free softmax policies
$`\pi_{\theta}(y)\propto\exp(\theta_{y})`$ with a suitable choice of
$`\mathcal{T}`$, and more generally for Fisher-nondegenerate compatible
parameterizations ([Yuan et al., 2022](#bib.bib57)), where
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}}`$ reflects function-approximation error.
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
provides the full assumptions and verification. The constants may depend
on $`\beta`$ and the reference policy $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$; in
particular, the clipping level $`M`$ depends on the bias
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}`$ and regularity of
$`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$.

###### Theorem 1 (Convergence guarantees of SPG, informal).

Assume (A1)–(A5) and $`\beta m\geq G^{2}`$. Let
$`\kappa\triangleq L/m`$. For the iterates of self-play policy gradients
(SPG, [3](#S4.E3 "In 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")):

Deterministic: with exact gradients and $`\gamma_{t}\equiv 1/(2L)`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{t})\leq(1-\gamma m/2)^{t}\,\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}}/m).
``` |  |

Stochastic: with $`\gamma_{t}=\Theta(1/(mt))`$ and a growing batch size
$`B_{t}=\Theta(t/m)`$, with probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{t})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\kappa^{2}/t^{2}+\kappa M^{2}/t^{2}+G^{2}M^{2}/(\beta m\cdot t)+(\varepsilon_{\rm PL}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}^{2})/m}}\right)\,.
``` |  |

We refer to
Propositions [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3 (Deterministic rates). ‣ C.1 Convergence Guarantees ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and
[4](#Thmproposition4 "Proposition 4 (Stochastic rates). ‣ C.1 Convergence Guarantees ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for complete statements and proofs. In the bound above, for the
stochastic case, the first term corresponds to deterministic
convergence, and the next two terms correspond to different noise terms:
the first comes from standard PL-SGD arguments ([Madden et al.,
2024](#bib.bib55)), whereas the second comes from a *moving
best-response*. The last term corresponds to an accumulated bias from an
inexact PL inequality or biased gradient updates. Before discussing the
assumptions, we demonstrate the source of the
$`1/(\beta m\cdot t)`$-term (moving best-response) in the stochastic
bound.

###### Lemma 1 (Descent Lemma I).

Assume (A1),(A2), and (A4). Let
$`\tilde{L}\triangleq L+G^{2}/(4\beta)`$. For the iterates of self-play
policy
gradients ([3](#S4.E3 "In 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")),
for any sequence $`(\gamma_{t})_{t\geq 0}`$ and for any $`t\geq 0`$, it
holds that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{t+1})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{t})-\gamma_{t}\big\langle\nabla J_{\beta}(\theta_{t};\pi_{t}),g_{t}\big\rangle+\tfrac{\gamma_{t}^{2}\tilde{L}}{2}\|g_{t}\|^{2}_{2}}+\sqrt{\tfrac{\gamma_{t}^{2}G^{2}\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{8\beta}}\,.`$ |  |

We refer to
Lemma [7](#Thmlemma7 "Lemma 7 (Descent Lemma I). ‣ C.1 Convergence Guarantees ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Convergence Guarantees ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for a complete proof. In the following, we provide a proof sketch to
demonstrate where the influence of moving baselines appears. In fact,
the additional term
$`\sqrt{\gamma_{t}^{2}\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}/\beta}`$ is exactly the
reason to introduce assumptions on $`\beta`$ and a growing batch size.

###### Sketch of the proof.

For simplicity of exposition, assume $`\mathcal{T}\equiv\mathrm{Id}`$.
Then the smoothness condition (A2) implies for
$`f_{t}(\theta)\equiv J_{\beta}(\theta;\pi_{t})\equiv V_{\beta}(\pi_{\theta};\pi_{t})`$
and $`\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}-\gamma_{t}g_{t}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{t}(\theta_{t+1})\leq f_{t}(\theta_{t})-\gamma_{t}\langle\nabla f_{t}(\theta_{t}),g_{t}\rangle+\tfrac{\gamma^{2}_{t}L}{2}\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.
``` |  |

Define $`f^{\star}_{t}=V^{\star}_{\beta}(\pi_{t})`$, and using a
relation $`S_{t}^{2}=f_{t}(\theta_{t})-f^{\star}_{t}`$ and simple
algebraic manipulations

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle S_{t+1}^{2}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq S_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}\langle\nabla f_{t}(\theta_{t}),g_{t}\rangle+\tfrac{\gamma_{t}^{2}L}{2}\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\underbrace{[f_{t+1}(\theta_{t+1})-f_{t}(\theta_{t+1})]+[f^{\star}_{t}-f^{\star}_{t+1}]}_{:=\mathcal{R}_{t}}\,.`$ |  |

For the term $`\mathcal{R}_{t}`$, we first notice that in the difference
$`f_{t+1}(\theta_{t+1})-f_{t}(\theta_{t+1})`$ the KL-terms cancels out,
thus we have
$`f_{t+1}(\theta_{t+1})-f_{t}(\theta_{t+1})=\mathcal{P}(\pi_{t+1}\succ\pi_{t+1})-\mathcal{P}(\pi_{t}\succ\pi_{t+1})`$,
which we denote as $`\mathcal{P}(\pi_{t+1}-\pi_{t}\succ\pi_{t+1})`$
using the linearity of expectation. For the final term, we use a
connection between $`f^{\star}_{t}\equiv V^{\star}_{\beta}(\pi_{t})`$
and a convex conjugate of entropy, thus
$`f^{\star}_{t+1}-f^{\star}_{t}\geq\mathcal{P}(\pi_{t+1}-\pi_{t}\succ\nu_{t+1})`$,
where $`\nu_{t+1}`$ is a best response against $`\pi_{t+1}`$. As a
result, using bilinearity and bi-Lipschitzness of preferences

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{t}\leq\mathcal{P}(\pi_{t+1}-\pi_{t}\succ\pi_{t+1}-\nu_{t+1})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lVert\pi_{t+1}-\pi_{t}\rVert_{1,\rho}\lVert\nu^{\star}_{t+1}-\pi_{t+1}\rVert_{1,\rho}\,.`$ |  |

Finally, using Assumption (A1), Pinsker’s inequality, and the connection
$`\beta\KL_{\rho}(\pi_{t+1}\|\nu^{\star}_{t+1})=S_{t+1}^{2}`$, we have
$`\mathcal{R}_{t}\leq(\gamma_{t}/2)\sqrt{G^{2}\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot S_{t+1}^{2}/\beta}`$.
Plugging this inequality into the inequality connecting $`S_{t+1}^{2}`$
and $`S_{t}^{2}`$ and solving the resulting quadratic inequality, we
conclude the statement. ∎

Why growing batches? Unlike standard analysis of SGD under the
PL-assumption on a fixed objective, the exploitability
$`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{t})=V_{\beta}(\pi_{t};\pi_{t})-V_{\beta}^{\star}(\pi_{t})`$
uses a *moving* best-response baseline. Controlling its drift yields an
intrinsic noise term of order $`\gamma_{t}\|\xi_{t}\|_{2}^{2}`$, which
motivates the need of a batch size $`B_{t}`$ increasing with $`t`$. We
also note that an implicit assumption about growing batch sizes appears
in the existing analysis of the extragradient method ([Zhou et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31)), where the noise term is scaled by
$`\sigma^{2}/\beta^{2}`$, with $`\sigma^{2}`$ denoting the gradient
variance. As remarked by [Madden et al. (2024)](#bib.bib55), similar
issues arise in the analysis of projected gradient descent in the
non-convex setting, and are typically resolved by a similar growing
mini-batch condition ([Ghadimi et al., 2016](#bib.bib59)) or by
variance-reduction techniques ([J. Reddi et al., 2016](#bib.bib60)).

Why $`\beta m\geq G^{2}`$? This condition ensures the best-response map
is well-contractive and one-step improvement is achievable; similar
“sufficient regularization” assumptions also appear in Mirror-Prox-style
analyses, see [Nemirovski (2004)](#bib.bib4) for a discussion. We note
that in the convex case ([Sokota et al., 2023](#bib.bib38)), it was
shown that a similar algorithm does not require such assumption;
however, in a parametrized non-convex setting, it is unclear whether
this is an artifact of the analysis or a necessity for the method’s
stability. We note that empirically the method may not achieves
monotonic improvements in suboptimality over the course of training in
the case of small $`\beta`$, making the last-iterate convergence
non-achievable; see
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Rock-Paper-Scissors ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for numerical experiments. We leave the complete identification of the
gap between these two settings as a direction for future work.

For the tabular softmax and Fisher-compatible parameterizations, the
following corollary summarizes the resulting iteration and sample
complexity. The rates depend on the regularity of the reference policy
under $`\rho`$: it controls the tails of the stochastic gradient
estimator and hence the clipping level $`M`$. This yields two regimes:
the context-free and the contextual settings.

###### Corollary 1 (SPG iteration and sample complexity, informal).

Fix confidence level $`\delta\in(0,1)`$ and target accuracy
$`\varepsilon\in(0,1)`$. Assume
$`\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}\!\big(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x)\big)}}\right]<\infty`$,
where
$`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x)\triangleq\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)`$.
Then SPG returns an
$`\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}})`$-VNW for the
$`\beta`$-regularized game in the following settings:

- (i)
  *Context-free, tabular softmax* ([Mei et al., 2020](#bib.bib29)). With
  clipping $`M=\Theta(\log(1/\varepsilon))`$ and
  $`\beta\gtrsim\beta_{\min}`$ (where $`\beta_{\min}`$ depends on the
  minimum reference mass),

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  N_{\mathrm{iter}}(\varepsilon)=T=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon^{-1}}}\right),\qquad N_{\mathrm{sample}}(\varepsilon)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon^{-2}}}\right).
  ``` |  |
- (ii)
  *Contextual, Fisher non-degenerate compatible parameterization* ([Yuan
  et al., 2022](#bib.bib57)). With clipping
  $`M=\Theta(1/\sqrt{\varepsilon})`$ and $`\beta\gtrsim\beta_{\min}`$
  (where $`\beta_{\min}`$ depends on the Fisher conditioning),

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  N_{\mathrm{iter}}(\varepsilon)=T=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon^{-2}}}\right),\qquad N_{\mathrm{sample}}(\varepsilon)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon^{-4}}}\right).
  ``` |  |

We refer to
Corollary [2](#Thmcorollary2 "Corollary 2 (SPG iteration and sample complexity: tabular softmax). ‣ Parametrization. ‣ C.3 Verification for the Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and
 [4](#Thmcorollary4 "Corollary 4 (SPG iteration and sample complexity: compatible Fisher parameterization). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for proofs and complete statements. We also notice that under a stronger
condition
$`\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x)]<\infty`$, one
can use a milder clipping level and recover the same improved rates in
both the contextual and context-free cases. For general context spaces,
however, $`\mathbb{E}[1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x)]<\infty`$ can be
substantially more restrictive than the log-moment condition.

## 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients

In this section, we propose a way to overcome a theoretical limitation
of the self-play policy-gradient (SPG) method by embedding it in the
Proximal Point (PP) method.

### 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method

A well-known method for computing a NE is the Proximal Point (PP) method
([Martinet, 1970](#bib.bib3); [Rockafellar, 1976](#bib.bib37)). At each
iteration, the PP method computes the NE of an auxiliary game that is
additionally regularized toward the previous iterate. To formally define
the iterates $`(\pi_{k})_{k\geq 0}`$, we initialize
$`\pi_{0}\equiv\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$, and for a PP step size $`\eta>0`$
we define $`\pi_{k+1}`$ as a solution to the following game:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\mu\in\Pi}\Bigl\{\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\pi\succ\mu)-(\beta/\eta)\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi_{k})+(\beta/\eta)\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\pi_{k})\Bigr\}\,,
``` |  | (7) |

which is equivalent to the original game, up to additional
regularization toward the previous iterate $`\pi_{k}`$. Alternatively,
we can view $`\pi_{k+1}`$ as a fixed point of the best-response
operator:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\pi_{k+1}\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}V_{k}(\pi;\pi_{k+1})\,,\qquad V_{k}(\pi;\mu)\triangleq V_{\beta}(\pi;\mu)+(\beta/\eta)\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi_{k}).
``` |  | (8) |

This approach often yields accelerated convergence rates for solving
games, but it is primarily a *conceptual* method rather than a
*practical* one, since exactly computing the NE of the regularized game
can be just as challenging as solving the original game. The key
observation is that the PP subproblems in
([8](#S5.E8 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
induce stronger effective regularization than the original game (when
$`\eta`$ is small), which allows us to address the theoretical
limitation of self-play policy gradients. This motivation is similar to
the motivation behind the Mirror-Prox algorithm ([Nemirovski,
2004](#bib.bib4)), which efficiently approximates a PP iteration by
performing two best-response steps for a well-chosen value of $`\eta`$.

PP residual and convergence guarantees. As discussed earlier, computing
([8](#S5.E8 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
exactly is challenging, especially in the function-approximation
setting. The exact iterates can equivalently be characterized by
$`\nabla_{\pi}V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\propto{\bf 1}`$, i.e., the
gradient vector becomes constant. This motivates the following
definition: we call $`\pi_{k+1}`$ an
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}`$-approximate PP update if

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\|\nabla_{\pi}V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}\,,
``` |  | (9) |

where $`\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}`$ is an expected (over
contexts) span seminorm of a vector; by definition, it equals zero on
constant vectors. Under this definition, it is possible to derive the
following guarantees; for the full statement and proof, see
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

###### Proposition 1 (Approximate PP convergence).

Assume that
([9](#S5.E9 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
holds for all $`k\geq 0`$. Then the iterates of approximate PP converge
toward $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ up to a residual floor:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\KL_{\rho}(\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\|\pi_{k})\leq(1+\eta/2)^{-k}\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\|\pi_{0})+2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}/\beta^{2}\,,
``` |  | (10) |

and similarly $`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{k})`$ and
$`\|\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}`$ decays at
the rate $`(1+\eta/2)^{-k}`$ with an additive
$`\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}})`$ term (see
Proposition [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2 (Convergence of approximate PP method). ‣ Equivalence with a mixed-reference value. ‣ B.3 Proximal Point Method ‣ Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in the
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for explicit constants).

Choice of $`\eta`$. Larger $`\eta`$ improves the outer contraction
factor in
([10](#S5.E10 "In Proposition 1 (Approximate PP convergence). ‣ 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")),
but it also weakens the proximal regularizer $`\beta/\eta`$ and can make
the inner games harder to solve. This is the same trade-off that
motivates the choice $`\eta=\mathcal{O}(\beta)`$ in Mirror-Prox-style
algorithms for this problem ([Cen et al., 2024](#bib.bib14)), which
resolves the inner problem using two iterations of the best-response
operator.

### 5.2 PP–SPG Method

We now describe how we implement the inexact PP step
([7](#S5.E7 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
using SPG, and summarize the resulting convergence guarantees.

Outer–inner structure. We set $`\pi_{0}=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$ and run
$`K`$ outer PP steps. At outer iteration $`k`$, we fix $`\pi_{k}`$ and
(approximately) solve the proximal subgame
([7](#S5.E7 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
by running SPG for $`T_{k}`$ inner steps. Denote

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\beta_{\mathrm{target}}\triangleq\beta/\eta,\qquad\lambda\triangleq\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=\beta\bigl(1+\frac{1}{\eta}\bigr),\qquad\tilde{\pi}_{k}\propto[\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}]^{\beta/\lambda}\times[\pi_{k}]^{\beta_{\mathrm{target}}/\lambda}.
``` |  |

The inner problem is exactly a $`\lambda`$-regularized preference game
anchored at $`\tilde{\pi}_{k}`$, i.e., it is of the same form as the
game analyzed for SPG in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

Inner objective for SPG. Let $`\pi_{\theta}`$ be a parametrized policy.
The $`\min`$-player objective against policy $`\pi`$ at the outer step
$`k`$ is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{k}(\theta;\pi)\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi_{\theta})+\beta\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi_{\theta}\|\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi_{\theta}\|\pi_{k}).
``` |  | (11) |

Self-play corresponds to using $`\pi=\pi_{\theta}`$ in $`J_{k}`$ (as in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")),
and updating parameters with stochastic gradients and an appropriately
chosen improvement operator $`\mathcal{T}_{k}\colon\Theta\to\Theta`$ to
ensure regularity:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta_{k,t+1}=\mathcal{T}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\theta_{k,t}-\gamma_{k,t}\,g_{k,t}}}\right),\qquad\pi_{k,t+1}=\pi_{\theta_{k,t+1}}.
``` |  | (12) |

At the end of the inner loop, we set
$`\pi_{k+1}\triangleq\pi_{k,T_{k}}`$. A concrete example of $`g_{k,t}`$
is the clipped pairwise REINFORCE estimator as in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
see
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Outer update. ‣ D.1 Algorithm Description ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for a formal definition and the pseudo-code.

Connection to COMAL ([Liu et al., 2024b](#bib.bib52)). Our method is
closely related in spirit to COMAL, which is motivated as a practical
implementation of the conceptual prox method for *unregularized*
preference games. COMAL proceeds by repeatedly solving a KL-regularized
subgame centered at a reference policy, and then updating the reference
to the newly computed iterate. In contrast, our goal is to compute the
Nash equilibrium of the *fixed* $`\beta`$-regularized game (anchored at
$`\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}`$), and we apply an (inexact) proximal-point outer
loop *on top of* this baseline regularization. Concretely, each PP outer
step adds an *additional* KL term toward the previous iterate, which
strengthens the effective regularization of the inner problem and yields
an improved contraction guarantee for the outer loop. This additional
proximal regularizer allows us to overcome the limitation of plain
self-play policy gradients in our setting and derive end-to-end
convergence rates.

### 5.3 Theoretical Guarantees

Approximate PP requires a bound on the residual
([9](#S5.E9 "In 5.1 (Approximate) Proximal Point Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")),
which is a gradient in *policy space*. Our inner solver operates in
*parameter space*. We bridge this gap via a gradient-compatibility
condition
(Assumption [11](#Thmassumption11 "Assumption 11 (Gradient compatibility). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
showing that, up to constants,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\|\nabla_{\pi}V_{k}(\pi_{\theta},\pi_{\theta})\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\ \lesssim\ \|\nabla_{\theta}J_{k}(\theta;\pi_{\theta})\|_{2}^{2}\,.
``` |  |

Notably, this assumption holds under both tabular softmax and
Fisher-compatible parameterizations. Combined with the smoothness of
$`J_{k}`$, this yields the key implication
(Lemma [24](#Thmlemma24 "Lemma 24 (From SPG progress to PP residual). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")):
if the inner loop returns $`\pi_{k+1}`$ with
$`\subopt^{\tilde{\pi}_{k}}_{\lambda}(\pi_{k+1})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$,
then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\|\nabla_{\pi}V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}})\ \ (\text{explicit in Appendix~\ref{app:pp-spg}}).
``` |  | (13) |

Therefore, controlling the *inner* SPG suboptimality suffices to control
the *outer* PP residual, which allows us to provide end-to-end
guarantees for the combined method. The main caveat in the analysis is
that, because our anchor sequence $`(\widetilde{\pi}_{k})_{k\geq 0}`$
depends on the previous iterate $`\pi_{k}`$, we cannot uniformly control
its behavior over all contexts, but we still can control the second
moment thanks to established convergence in
$`\lVert\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}`$.

###### Theorem 2 (PP–SPG sample complexity, informal).

Fix arbitrary $`\beta\leq 1`$, confidence $`\delta\in(0,1)`$, and target
accuracy $`\varepsilon\in(0,1)`$. Run PP–SPG for
$`K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)`$ outer steps, and set the inner target
accuracy $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}=\Theta(\varepsilon)`$. Then, with
probability at least $`1-\delta`$, the output $`\pi_{K}`$ satisfies
$`\subopt_{\beta}(\pi_{K})\leq\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}})`$.
Moreover, the combined method achieves the same order of total iteration
and sample complexity as in
Corollary [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1 (SPG iteration and sample complexity, informal). ‣ 4.1 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

See
Corollaries [7](#Thmcorollary7 "Corollary 7 (Convergence guarantees for Softmax parametrization). ‣ D.4 Verification for Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and [8](#Thmcorollary8 "Corollary 8 (Convergence guarantees for Fisher compatible parameterization). ‣ D.5 Verification for Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
in
Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for full statements and proofs. The contextual rate improves to
$`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-2})`$ under stronger assumptions
on $`\mathcal{T}`$ and $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$. With PP alone, we control
only a second log-moment, so gradients may be heavy-tailed. SVRG-style
variance reduction could further reduce sampling complexity ([J. Reddi
et al., 2016](#bib.bib60)); we leave this direction to future work.

### 5.4 Nash Prox: Practical Deep Learning Implementation

Next, we provide a practical implementation, which we call
$`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$. We consider the following policies: an online
policy $`\pi_{t}`$ with parameters $`\theta_{t}`$, a *target policy*
$`\pi^{\mathrm{target}}_{t}`$ with parameters
$`\theta^{\mathrm{target}}_{t}`$, and a fixed reference policy
$`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$. Next, we define the following IPO-style loss
function
(cf. ([6](#S4.E6 "In 4 Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")))

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\textstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{Prox}}(\theta;\pi^{\mathrm{target}},\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\,y,y^{\prime}\sim\mathtt{sg}(\pi_{\theta}(\cdot|x))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell_{\theta}(x,y)-\ell_{\theta}(x,y^{\prime})-\tfrac{p-1/2}{\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\,,
``` |  | (14) |

where
$`\ell_{\theta}(x,y)\triangleq(\beta/\lambda)\cdot\log(\pi_{\theta}(y|x)/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y|x))+(\beta_{\mathrm{target}}/\lambda)\cdot\log(\pi_{\theta}(y|x)/\pi^{\mathrm{target}}(y|x))`$
for $`\lambda\triangleq\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}`$, and $`p`$ is an
estimator of $`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)`$. As explained
before, the stochastic gradient of this loss function corresponds to our
method, but it is easier to implement in practice. To implement the
algorithm exactly as we discussed in
Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 PP–SPG Method ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
we need to update the target policy with the weights of the online
policy every $`T_{k}`$ steps, where $`T_{k}`$ defines the update
schedule. However, following a very common strategy in deep
reinforcement learning ([Mnih et al., 2015](#bib.bib22); [Lillicrap et
al., 2016](#bib.bib45)), we employ a more practical and elegant approach
and update the target with an exponential moving average instead,
resulting in the following updates:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\cdot\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{Prox}}(\theta_{t};\pi^{\mathrm{target}}_{t},\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\,,\qquad\theta^{\mathrm{target}}_{t+1}=(1-\kappa)\cdot\theta^{\mathrm{target}}_{t}+\kappa\cdot\theta_{t+1}\,,
``` |  |

where $`\alpha_{t}`$ is a learning rate and the parameter
$`\kappa\in[0,1]`$ implicitly controls the number of steps for one
proximal update. Thus, we approximate the solution of one proximal
subproblem with $`T_{k}\approx 1/\kappa`$ gradient steps. Also, we found
it empirically beneficial to anneal the value of $`\kappa`$ as
$`\kappa_{t}=1/(c\cdot t+1)`$ for some value of $`c`$, where
$`K\approx 1/c`$ corresponds to the approximate number of outer PP
iterates.

## 6 Experiments

We use the simple contextual dueling bandit problem as an initial
experiment to study the deep learning implementation of
$`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$. We refer to
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Rock-Paper-Scissors ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for an additional small-scale experiment on the Rock-Paper-Scissors
game.

Game definition. Let us fix a number of actions $`Y\geq 2`$ and a
positive integer $`r\geq 1`$. We consider a dueling bandit game with a
context space $`\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}`$ and an action space
$`\mathcal{Y}=\{1,\ldots,Y\}`$. Preference probabilities are defined as
follows

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}|x)\triangleq\sigma(A_{y,y^{\prime}}-A_{y^{\prime},y})\,,
``` |  |

for $`A\triangleq U\Theta_{x}V^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}`$, where
$`U\in\mathbb{R}^{Y\times r}`$ and $`V\in\mathbb{R}^{Y\times r}`$ are
fixed matrices, $`\Theta_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}`$ is a
corresponding context matrix, and $`\sigma(\cdot)`$ is a sigmoid
function. This type of dueling bandit instance is a generalization of a
low-rank linear bandit problem. Notice that for any $`r\geq 2`$ this
problem does not admit a Bradley-Terry model. The distribution over
contexts $`\rho`$ is assumed to be a standard Gaussian random matrix
(i.e., elements of $`\Theta_{x}`$ are i.i.d. with distribution
$`\mathcal{N}(0,1)`$). We aim to find a policy
$`\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$ that approximates a
$`\beta`$-regularized VNW. We refer to
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for more details on the setup.

![Refer to
caption](2505.19731v2/best_configs_exploitability_over_time.png)

Figure 1: Comparison of $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ (in violet) with
baseline methods. Suboptimality is averaged over 25 random seeds; shaded
regions indicate standard error.

Results. We compare our method with the following baselines: Online
IPO ([Calandriello et al., 2024](#bib.bib28)), Online DPO ([Guo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib32)), EGPO ([Zhou et al., 2025](#bib.bib31)), Nash
MD ([Munos et al., 2023](#bib.bib25)), and $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$. The
results are presented in
Figure [1](#S6.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 6 Experiments ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").
We can observe that $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ with an adaptive
$`\kappa_{t}=1/(0.3\cdot t+1)`$ and
$`\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=10\times\beta`$ outperforms all the baselines.
We also observe that the two-step stabilization procedure of EGPO is
insufficient to stabilize training in the function approximation
setting, while Online DPO diverges because it is not designed to solve
the underlying preference game in the first place. Additionally, we
observe non-monotonous behavior of suboptimality for all the baselines,
including online IPO. *This empirical fact is a direct demonstration of
a necessity of the stability condition on $`\beta`$ for last-iterate
convergence guarantees, which is typically relies on contraction
principle.* In
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
we provide an additional ablation study on the choice of $`\kappa`$ on
the optimization procedure as well as a comparison between hard and soft
updates.

### 6.1 LLM Alignment

Experiment setup. For our LLM-based experiments, we use the Gemma-3-4B
([Team et al., 2025](#bib.bib75)) pretrained model checkpoints and train
on the RLHFlow ([Dong et al., 2024](#bib.bib48)) datasets for all the
analysis. In particular, we first perform SFT on the RLHFlow SFT dataset
([RLHFlow Team, 2024c](#bib.bib42)) and then all our NLHF experiments
use LoRA adapters ([Hu et al., 2022](#bib.bib76)) of rank 16 with
$`\alpha=32`$ on the resulting checkpoint, using a subset of RLHFlow
Prompt collection ([RLHFlow Team, 2024b](#bib.bib43)). The pairwise
judge model is Gemma-2-2B trained via the Robust Reward Models ([Liu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib40)) method. All the experiments were performed using
the TRL library ([von Werra et al., 2020](#bib.bib47)).

Baselines. We compare the practical version of $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$
against Online DPO ([Guo et al., 2024](#bib.bib32)) and Online IPO
([Calandriello et al., 2024](#bib.bib28)). We also considered
$`\mathtt{NashMD}`$ ([Munos et al., 2023](#bib.bib25)) and EGPO ([Zhou
et al., 2025](#bib.bib31)), but did not include them in our experiments
because their current implementations are prohibitively slow in our
setting (we estimate on the order of $`10^{3}`$ GPU-hours per
configuration). For $`\mathtt{NashMD}`$, the main bottleneck is the need
to sample from geometric mixtures, while for EGPO the reference
implementation incurs substantial overhead due to repeated optimizer
off-loading and reloading. Additional details on baselines and
hyperparameters are provided in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

Results. We report in
Table [1](#S6.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 6.1 LLM Alignment ‣ 6 Experiments ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
the pairwise win-rates between the different methods on a held-out test
set of prompts for the separate Gemma3-27B-IT judge model. We observe
that $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ outperforms all the baselines. From the
implementation side, the main difference between $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$
and Online IPO is the use of additional regularization with respect to a
target model, and our results show that this regularization can be
valuable.

| Win rate | SFT | Online DPO | Online IPO | $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| SFT | $`-`$ | $`0.0674{\scriptstyle\pm 0.011}`$ | $`0.0636{\scriptstyle\pm 0.011}`$ | $`0.0567{\scriptstyle\pm 0.010}`$ |
| Online DPO | $`\bm{0.9326}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.011}`$ | $`-`$ | $`\bm{0.5283}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ | $`0.4656{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ |
| Online IPO | $`\bm{0.9364}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.011}`$ | $`0.4717{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ | $`-`$ | $`0.4373{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ |
| $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ | $`\bm{0.9433}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.010}`$ | $`\bm{0.5344}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ | $`\bm{0.5627}{\scriptstyle\pm 0.017}`$ | $`-`$ |

Table 1: Pairwise win rates (mean $`\pm`$ 99.9%-confidence intervals).
Statistically significant wins are in bold. Confidence intervals are in
a smaller font size. Row/column for $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ is
highlighted.

## 7 Conclusion

In this work, we study the problem of computing Nash equilibria in
KL-regularized preference games that arise in NLHF, where the
regularizer enforces proximity to a reference policy. We first analyze a
simple self-play policy gradient method under general policy
parameterizations and establish last-iterate, high-probability
convergence guarantees. The analysis also highlights a structural
difficulty of self-play, namely that the best-response baseline changes
across iterations and may force a stringent effective-regularization
condition. To remove this restriction, we embed self-play within a
proximal point scheme, whose proximal subgames induce stronger
regularization and yield last-iterate convergence without requiring a
lower bound on the original regularization strength. Finally, we
instantiate this framework with practical stochastic policy-gradient
updates and EMA-based stabilization mechanisms, resulting in a concrete
algorithm, $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$, that matches state-of-the-art
empirical performance while retaining guarantees in the parameterized
setting.

## Impact Statement

This paper aims to advance methods for aligning learning systems with
human feedback without assuming transitive preferences. Potential
societal impacts include improved safety and robustness of interactive
AI systems, and risks include mis-specifying human preferences or
reinforcing annotator biases.

## Appendix A Notation

|  Symbol |  Meaning |
|----|----|
|  $`\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}`$ |  Context space and finite action set; $`|\mathcal{Y}|=Y`$. |
|  $`\rho`$ |  Distribution over contexts $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$. |
|  $`\pi,\mu`$ |  Policies $`\mathcal{X}\to\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$; $`\pi(y|x)`$ is action-probability. |
|  $`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)`$ |  Preference probability; satisfies $`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}|x)+\mathcal{P}(y^{\prime}\succ y|x)=1`$. |
|  $`{\bf P}_{x}`$ |  Preference matrix at context $`x`$: $`({\bf P}_{x})_{y,y^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}|x)`$. |
|  $`\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu\mid x)`$ |  Contextual preference: $`\pi(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\mu(x)`$. |
|  $`\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)`$ |  Overall preference: $`\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\pi(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\mu(x)]`$. |
|  $`\langle f,g\rangle_{\rho}`$ |  Inner product: $`\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle]`$. |
|  $`\|\cdot\|_{1,\rho},\ \|\cdot\|_{\infty,\rho}`$ |  $`\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|\cdot\|_{1}^{2}]}`$ and $`\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|\cdot\|_{\infty}^{2}]}`$. |
|  $`\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}`$ |  Span seminorm in context: $`\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{sp}}^{2}]}`$. |
|  $`\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\mu)`$ |  Contextual KL: $`\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\KL(\pi(x)\|\mu(x))]`$. |
|  $`\widetilde{\pi}`$ |  Anchor/reference policy (generic). |
|  $`\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi\succ\mu)`$ |  $`\lambda`$-regularized payoff: $`\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\widetilde{\pi})`$. |
|  $`\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)`$ |  Regularized suboptimality: $`\tfrac{1}{2}-\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi\succ\mu)`$. |
|  $`V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)`$ |  Regularized value: $`\mathcal{P}(\mu\succ\pi)+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})`$. |
|  $`\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$ |  Best response: $`\argmin_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)`$. |
|  $`V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$ |  Best-response value: $`\min_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)`$. |
|  $`\beta`$ |  Regularization strength of the “outer/original” preference game. |
|  $`\eta`$ |  Proximal-point (PP) step size; $`\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=\beta/\eta`$ is the proximal penalty. |
|  $`\pi_{t}`$ |  Outer PP iterate (policy at PP step $`t`$). |
|  $`V_{t}(\pi,\mu)`$ |  PP inner value: $`V_{\beta}(\pi,\mu)+(\beta/\eta)\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi_{t})`$. |
|  $`\zeta_{t+1}`$ |  PP residual: $`\nabla_{\pi}V_{t}(\pi_{t+1},\pi_{t+1})`$. |
|  $`\varepsilon`$ |  PP inner-solve accuracy target: $`\|\zeta_{t+1}\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq\varepsilon`$. |
|  $`\theta,\pi_{\theta}`$ |  Parametric policy: parameter $`\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ with induced policy $`\pi_{\theta}`$. |
|  $`J^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\theta;\pi)`$ |  Best-response objective: $`\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\pi_{\theta})+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi_{\theta}\|\widetilde{\pi})`$. |
|  $`g_{t},\xi_{t},b_{t},B_{t}`$ |  Stochastic gradient, noise, bias, and mini-batch size in SPG. |
|  $`m_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}}`$ |  (Approx.) PL constant and additive PL slack in Assumption [4](#Thmassumption4 "Assumption 4 (Approximate Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality). ‣ Parametrization assumptions. ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"). |
|  |  |

Table 2: Notation used in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")–[D](#A4 "Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

The space of discrete probability measure of dimension $`d`$ is denoted
by
$`\Delta_{d}=\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:p_{i}\geq 0,\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}=1\}`$.
In the same manner, we associate for any set $`\mathcal{Y}`$ of size
$`Y=|\mathcal{Y}|`$ $`\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$ with $`\Delta_{Y}`$. Given
two discrete probability measure $`p,q\in\Delta_{d}`$, let us define the
KL-divergence between them as
$`\KL(p\|q)\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}\log(p_{i}/q_{i})`$. For two
vectors $`x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$, we define an inner product as
$`\langle x,y\rangle\triangleq\sum_{i=1}x_{i}y_{i}`$.

## Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method

In this section, we analyze the exact and approximate proximal point
methods.

### B.1 Preference Properties

We work in the contextual dueling bandit setting
$`(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{P},\rho)`$ described in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Setting ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").
The action space $`\mathcal{Y}`$ is finite with
$`|\mathcal{Y}|=Y\geq 2`$, and in each round, a context
$`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ is drawn from $`\rho`$. We assume that the context
space is finite or countable for simplicity. We define a policy space
$`\Pi`$ as a functional space with a domain $`\mathcal{X}`$ and a
codomain $`\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$.

For every context $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ and actions
$`y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}`$, we are given preference probabilities
$`\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\in[0,1]`$ that satisfy the
symmetry assumption

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)+\mathcal{P}(y^{\prime}\succ y\mid x)=1\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},\,y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}.
``` |  |

For each context $`x`$, we define the preference matrix
$`{\bf P}_{x}\in[0,1]^{|\mathcal{Y}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ with entries
$`({\bf P}_{x})_{y,y^{\prime}}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)`$.
For policies $`\pi,\mu\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$, we
define the contextual preference

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu\mid x)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi(x),\,y^{\prime}\sim\mu(x)}\big[\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\big]=\pi(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\mu(x)\,,
``` |  |

and the overall preference

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\big[\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu\mid x)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\big[\pi(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\mu(x)\big]\,.
``` |  |

More generally, for measurable functions
$`v,u\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ (not necessarily
probability distributions), we extend the notation as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(v\succ u\mid x)\triangleq v(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}u(x),\qquad\mathcal{P}(v\succ u)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\big[v(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}u(x)\big].
``` |  | (15) |

On such functions, we use the following inner product and norms:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\langle f,g\rangle_{\rho}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle]\,,\ \|f\|_{1,\rho}\triangleq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|f(x)\|_{1}^{2}]}\,,\ \|f\|_{\infty,\rho}\triangleq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|f(x)\|_{\infty}^{2}]}\,.
``` |  |

For a function $`g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$, we
extend the span seminorm by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\|g\|_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\big[\|g(x)\|_{\mathrm{sp}}^{2}\big],
``` |  |

where $`\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{sp}}`$ on $`\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ is
the span seminorm introduced in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Setting ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

For any functional $`\mathcal{F}`$ defined on a policy space $`\Pi`$, we
define its derivative at $`\pi`$ as the function
$`\nabla\mathcal{F}(\pi)\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$
satisfying the directional derivative identity

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathcal{F}(\pi+th)\Big|_{t=0}=\langle\nabla\mathcal{F}(\pi),h\rangle_{\rho}\qquad\forall h\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|},\langle h(x),{\bf 1}\rangle=0\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X}\,.
``` |  |

In particular, it can be characterized as
$`(\nabla\mathcal{F}(\pi))(x,y)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\pi(y|x)}\mathcal{F}(\pi)`$
for $`\rho`$-almost all $`x`$ and all $`y\in\mathcal{Y}`$.

Finally, for policies $`\pi,\mu`$ we set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\mu)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\KL(\pi(x)\|\mu(x))],
``` |  |

and we recall Pinsker’s inequality applied pointwise in $`x`$:

|     |                                                       |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \|\pi(x)-\mu(x)\|_{1}^{2}\leq 2\,\KL(\pi(x)\|\mu(x)),
       ```                                                    |     |

which implies

|     |                                                       |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \|\pi-\mu\|_{1,\rho}^{2}\leq 2\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\mu).
       ```                                                    |     | (16) |

We assume throughout that all policies have full support on
$`\mathcal{Y}`$ for $`\rho`$-almost all $`x`$, so that the expressions
involving $`\log\pi(y|x)`$ are well-defined.

###### Lemma 2 (Lipschitz property of preferences).

Let $`u,v\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ be measurable
functions such that $`\langle v(x),{\bf 1}\rangle=0`$ for all
$`x\in\mathcal{X}`$. Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\mathcal{P}(u\succ v)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\|u\|_{1,\rho}\,\|v\|_{1,\rho},\qquad|\mathcal{P}(v\succ u)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\|u\|_{1,\rho}\,\|v\|_{1,\rho}.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

Fix any $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$. Using
$`\sum_{y^{\prime}}v(x,y^{\prime})=0`$, for any $`y\in\mathcal{Y}`$ we
can write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
({\bf P}_{x}v(x))_{y}=\sum_{y^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)v(x,y^{\prime})=\sum_{y^{\prime}}(\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)-1/2)v(x,y^{\prime}).
``` |  |

Hence

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|({\bf P}_{x}v(x))_{y}|\leq\max_{y^{\prime}}|\mathcal{P}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)-1/2|\sum_{y^{\prime}}|v(x,y^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|v(x)\|_{1},
``` |  |

which implies
$`\|{\bf P}_{x}v(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|v(x)\|_{1}`$. Therefore,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\mathcal{P}(u\succ v\mid x)|=|u(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}v(x)|\leq\|u(x)\|_{1}\|{\bf P}_{x}v(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|u(x)\|_{1}\|v(x)\|_{1}.
``` |  |

Taking expectation over $`x\sim\rho`$ and applying Cauchy–Schwarz,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\mathcal{P}(u\succ v)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\|u(x)\|_{1}\|v(x)\|_{1}]\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\|u\|_{1,\rho}\,\|v\|_{1,\rho}.
``` |  |

The bound for $`|\mathcal{P}(v\succ u)|`$ is obtained in the same way. ∎

### B.2 Regularized Preference and Value

Fix a reference policy $`\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi`$ and $`\lambda>0`$. We
consider the contextual $`\lambda`$-regularized preference game

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\min_{\mu\in\Pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi\succ\mu)\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\widetilde{\pi})}}\right\}.
``` |  | (17) |

The (contextual) regularized suboptimality is defined as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi\succ\mu),\qquad\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi)\triangleq\max_{\mu\in\Pi}\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu).
``` |  |

We introduce the regularized value function

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\mu\succ\pi)+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})\,,
``` |  | (18) |

and denote

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu),\qquad V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\triangleq\min_{\pi\in\Pi}V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)\,.
``` |  |

Note that $`\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$ might be
non-unique on $`\rho`$-zero measure sets, but
$`V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$ is always well-defined.

###### Lemma 3 (Exploitability gap via value function).

For any policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$, it holds

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi)=V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\pi)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\pi)\,.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

By definition of $`\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)`$ and
using $`\mathcal{P}(\mu\succ\pi)=1-\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\mu)+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\widetilde{\pi})=V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)-\tfrac{1}{2}-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\widetilde{\pi})\,.
``` |  |

Maximizing over $`\mu`$ yields

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi)=\max_{\mu}\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)=\max_{\mu}\bigl(V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)-\tfrac{1}{2}-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\mu\|\widetilde{\pi})\bigr).
``` |  |

In particular, evaluating at $`\mu=\pi`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\pi)=\frac{1}{2}+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi}).
``` |  |

On the other hand,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\pi)=\min_{\nu\in\Pi}\bigl(\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\nu)+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\nu\|\widetilde{\pi})\bigr),
``` |  |

so

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\pi)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\pi)=\max_{\nu\in\Pi}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi})-\mathcal{P}(\pi\succ\nu)-\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\nu\|\widetilde{\pi})\bigr),
``` |  |

where the right-hand side exactly matches the definition of
$`\subopt^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi)`$. ∎

###### Lemma 4 (Strong convexity in $`\pi`$ with respect to $`\KL_{\rho}`$).

Fix $`\mu,\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi`$. Then, for any
$`\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)-V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)=\langle\nabla_{\pi}V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\rho}+\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi^{\prime})\,,
``` |  | (19) |

where $`\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)`$,
defined as a Fréchet derivative of
$`V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)`$ with respect to the
first argument, is given element-wise by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\big(\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)\big)(x,y)\triangleq\big({\bf P}_{x}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mu(x)\big)_{y}+\lambda\Big(1+\log\frac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\Big),
``` |  |

In particular, if
$`\pi^{\prime}=\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\in\argmin_{\pi}V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)`$,
then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(\pi,\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\geq\lambda\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)).
``` |  |

###### Proof.

Write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\Big[\mu(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\pi(x)+\lambda f_{x}(\pi(x))\Big],\qquad f_{x}(p)\triangleq\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}p_{y}\log\frac{p_{y}}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}.
``` |  |

Fix $`\mu,\widetilde{\pi}`$ and $`\pi^{\prime}`$. Let
$`h\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ be any direction and
consider

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Phi(t)\triangleq V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime}+th,\mu).
``` |  |

For each $`x`$ and $`y`$, the derivative of
$`p\mapsto p\log\frac{p}{\tau}`$ at $`p=p^{\prime}`$ is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p}\Big(p\log\frac{p}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\Big)\Big|_{p=p^{\prime}}=\log\frac{p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}+1.
``` |  |

Thus, pointwise in $`x`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Big[\mu(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}(\pi^{\prime}(x)+th(x))\Big]_{t=0}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mu(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}h(x),`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Big[\lambda f_{x}(\pi^{\prime}(x)+th(x))\Big]_{t=0}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{y}h(x,y)\Big(\log\frac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}+1\Big).`$ |  |

Therefore

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\Big[\sum_{y}\Big(({\bf P}_{x}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mu(x))_{y}+\lambda\big(1+\log\tfrac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\big)\Big)h(x,y)\Big]=\langle\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),h\rangle_{\rho},
``` |  |

with

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\big(\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)\big)(x,y)=({\bf P}_{x}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mu(x))_{y}+\lambda\Big(1+\log\tfrac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\Big).
``` |  |

Now consider the difference

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\Big[\mu(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\big(\pi(x)-\pi^{\prime}(x)\big)+\lambda\big(f_{x}(\pi(x))-f_{x}(\pi^{\prime}(x))\big)\Big].
``` |  |

Similarly,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\langle\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\rho}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\Big[\mu(x)^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}{\bf P}_{x}\big(\pi(x)-\pi^{\prime}(x)\big)+\lambda\sum_{y}\big(1+\log\tfrac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\big)\big(\pi(y|x)-\pi^{\prime}(y|x)\big)\Big].
``` |  |

Subtracting, we isolate the Bregman term:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)-\langle\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\rho}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\quad=\lambda\,\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\Big[f_{x}(\pi(x))-f_{x}(\pi^{\prime}(x))-\sum_{y}\big(1+\log\tfrac{\pi^{\prime}(y|x)}{\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)}\big)\big(\pi(y|x)-\pi^{\prime}(y|x)\big)\Big].`$ |  |

Fix $`x`$ and abbreviate $`p_{y}=\pi(y|x)`$,
$`p^{\prime}_{y}=\pi^{\prime}(y|x)`$, $`\tau_{y}=\widetilde{\pi}(y|x)`$.
Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{x}(p)=\sum_{y}p_{y}\log\frac{p_{y}}{\tau_{y}},\qquad\frac{\partial f_{x}}{\partial p_{y}}(p^{\prime})=\log\frac{p^{\prime}_{y}}{\tau_{y}}+1.
``` |  |

The inner expression is the Bregman divergence of $`f_{x}`$ at
$`p\|p^{\prime}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{x}(p)-f_{x}(p^{\prime})-\sum_{y}\frac{\partial f_{x}}{\partial p_{y}}(p^{\prime})(p_{y}-p^{\prime}_{y}).
``` |  |

A standard calculation gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{x}(p)-f_{x}(p^{\prime})-\sum_{y}\frac{\partial f_{x}}{\partial p_{y}}(p^{\prime})(p_{y}-p^{\prime}_{y})=\sum_{y}p_{y}\log\frac{p_{y}}{p^{\prime}_{y}}=\KL(p\|p^{\prime}).
``` |  |

Thus

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)-\langle\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\rho}=\lambda\,\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\KL(\pi(x)\|\pi^{\prime}(x))]=\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi^{\prime}),
``` |  |

which gives
([19](#A2.E19 "In Lemma 4 (Strong convexity in 𝜋 with respect to KL_𝜌). ‣ B.2 Regularized Preference and Value ‣ Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")).

For the final claim, let
$`\pi^{\prime}=\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$ be a
minimizer of $`V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\cdot,\mu)`$. First-order
optimality implies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\langle\nabla_{\pi}V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\rho}\geq 0\qquad\forall\pi\in\Pi.
``` |  |

Plugging $`\pi^{\prime}`$ into
([19](#A2.E19 "In Lemma 4 (Strong convexity in 𝜋 with respect to KL_𝜌). ‣ B.2 Regularized Preference and Value ‣ Appendix B Analysis of Proximal Point Method ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
and using
$`V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi^{\prime},\mu)=V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)`$
yields

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\lambda}(\pi,\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\geq\lambda\,\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\pi^{\prime})=\lambda\KL_{\rho}(\pi\|\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu))\,,
``` |  |

as claimed. ∎

###### Lemma 5 (Smoothness of the best-response value).

Fix a reference policy $`\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi`$ and $`\lambda>0`$.
Then, for any policies $`\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(\mu-\mu^{\prime}\succ\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu))\leq V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{P}(\mu-\mu^{\prime}\succ\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime}))\,.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

Note that the lower bound follows automatically from the upper bound by
multiplying both sides by $`-1`$ and renaming $`\mu`$ and
$`\mu^{\prime}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{P}(\mu-\mu^{\prime}\succ\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime}))\iff V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime})-V^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu)\geq\mathcal{P}(\mu^{\prime}-\mu\succ\nu^{\widetilde{\pi},\star}_{\lambda}(\mu^{\prime}))
``` |  |

Thus, it is enough to prove only an upper bound. Fix an arbitrary
context $`x\in\mathrm{supp}(\rho)`$ and consider the regularizer
$`q\in\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}`$ as

|     |                                                              |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \varphi_{x}(q)\triangleq\lambda\KL(q\|\widetilde{\pi}(x))\,,
       ```                                                           |     |

and we define its convex conjugate $`\varphi_{x}^{*}`$ on
$`g\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{Y}}`$ as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\varphi_{x}^{*}(g)\triangleq\sup_{q\in\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}}\big\{\langle g,q\rangle-\varphi_{x}(q)\big\}=\sup_{q\in\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}}\big\{\langle g,q\rangle-\lambda\KL(q\|\widetilde{\pi}(x))\big\}.
``` |  |

We note that the convex conjugate is always a convex function. As a
result, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
````
