````math
$`\mu_{\min}(x)=\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\mu(y|x)`$. Then, the following
bound holds

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}}}\right|`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert\log\pi(x)-\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,,`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}}}\right)}}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 4\lVert\log\pi-\log\mu\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\,.`$ |  |

Additionally, the following bound holds

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\pi_{\min}(x)}}}\right)}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{\min}(x)}}}\right)}}\right]+8\lVert\log\pi-\log\mu\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\,.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

First, we use the relation between the span seminorm and a range:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\log\pi(x)-\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}-\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}}}\right)\,.
``` |  |

We notice that for any pair of policies it holds
$`\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}\geq 0`$ and
$`\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}\leq 0`$, thus we
have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)},-\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}}}\right\}\leq 2\lVert\log\pi(x)-\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,.
``` |  |

Finally, we note that the left-hand side is equal to
$`\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}|\log\frac{\pi(y|x)}{\mu(y|x)}|`$. Taking a
square and expectation over $`x\sim\rho`$ concludes the first part of
the proof.

The second part of the proof follows from a bound

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\log\pi(x)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\log\pi(x)-\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\lVert\log\pi(x)-\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}+\lVert\log\mu(x)\rVert_{\infty}\,,
``` |  |

which follows from the triangle inequality and the first part of the
lemma, squaring both sides, taking expectation over $`x\sim\rho`$, and
using a bound $`(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}`$. ∎

In particular, this lemma allows us to show that if a policy $`\pi_{k}`$
generated by the PP–SPG algorithm satisfies
$`\lVert\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq C`$
for some constant $`C>0`$, then the mixed anchor $`\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$
for the next step satisfies
Assumption [7](#Thmassumption7 "Assumption 7 (Reference-policy regularity). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").
Next, we prove the result that connects the inexact solution to the
inner problem to PP residuals needed to establish the convergence of the
outer algorithm.

### D.4 Verification for Softmax Parametrization

In this section, we show that context-free softmax parametrization
satisfies
Assumptions [11](#Thmassumption11 "Assumption 11 (Gradient compatibility). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and
[12](#Thmassumption12 "Assumption 12 (Uniform inner-game regularity). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

###### Lemma 29.

Consider a context-free setting and assume that
$`\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}`$
for all $`k<K`$. Then for the next step $`K`$ it holds

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{K,\min})\leq\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+\frac{1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{(1+\eta)\beta}\,,
``` |  |

or, equivalently,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\frac{1}{\widetilde{\pi}_{K,\min}}\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}\cdot\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{(1+\eta)\beta}}}\right)\,.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

We start from the first inequality of
Lemma [28](#Thmlemma28 "Lemma 28. ‣ D.3 Assumption Verification ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
with $`\pi=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$ and $`\mu=\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}-\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\lVert\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}-\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,.
``` |  |

For the left-hand side, we apply a reverse triangle inequality and a
fact
$`\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}\rVert_{\infty}=\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min})`$.
For the right-hand side, we use a definition of a geometric mixture.
Overall, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min})\leq\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+\frac{2}{1+\eta}\lVert\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,.
``` |  |

Next, we use
Lemma [27](#Thmlemma27 "Lemma 27 (Uniform regularity of PP iterates). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
to bound the second term. Thus, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min})\leq\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+\frac{1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{(1+\eta)\beta}\,.
``` |  |

Exponentiating both sides concludes the proof. ∎

Also, we will need the following technical lemma.

###### Lemma 30.

Let $`x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$, then for any $`\pi\in\Delta_{d}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert H(\pi)x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\geq\pi_{\min}\cdot\lVert x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,,
``` |  |

where $`\pi_{\min}=\min_{i\in[d]}\pi(i)`$ and
$`H(\pi)=\mathrm{diag}(\pi)-\pi\pi^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}`$.

###### Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that $`\pi(i)>0`$ for any
$`i\in[d]`$, otherwise the statement trivially holds.

First, we notice that
$`\lVert x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}=1/2\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max_{i}x_{i}-\min_{i}x_{i}}}\right)`$
and define $`i_{\max}=\argmax_{i}x_{i}`$ and
$`i_{\min}=\argmin_{i}x_{i}`$.

Then, we notice that $`H(\pi)x=\pi\odot(x-\mu{\bf 1})`$, where
$`\mu=\pi^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}x`$ is a mean of $`x`$ under
$`\pi`$. Since $`\pi`$ is a positive measure, we have
$`x_{i_{\max}}\geq\mu`$ and $`x_{i_{\min}}\leq\mu`$, therefore

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\max_{i}\pi(i)(x_{i}-\mu)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\pi(i_{\max})\cdot\underbrace{(x_{i_{\max}}-\mu)}_{\geq 0}\geq\pi_{\min}\cdot(x_{i_{\max}}-\mu)\,`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\min_{i}\pi(i)(x_{i}-\mu)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\pi(i_{\min})\underbrace{(x_{i_{\min}}-\mu)}_{\leq 0}\leq\pi_{\min}\cdot(x_{i_{\min}}-\mu)\,,`$ |  |

thus

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\lVert H(\pi)x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(1/2)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max_{i}\pi(i)(x_{i}-\mu)-\min_{i}\pi(i)(x_{i}-\mu)}}\right)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq(1/2)\pi_{\min}(x_{i_{\max}}-\mu-x_{i_{\min}}+\mu)=\pi_{\min}\lVert x\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}.`$ |  |

∎

###### Corollary 7 (Convergence guarantees for Softmax parametrization).

Let $`\theta\mapsto\pi_{\theta}`$ be a context-free softmax
parametrization and $`(\mathcal{T}_{k})_{k\geq 0}`$ a sequence of
improvement operators
$`\mathcal{T}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{T}^{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}_{\tau_{k,0}}`$
for $`\tau_{k,0}`$ equal to $`\tau_{0}`$ with
$`\widetilde{\pi}=\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$, as defined in
Lemma [16](#Thmlemma16 "Lemma 16. ‣ C.3.2 Improvement Operator and Uniform PL ‣ C.3 Verification for the Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
with $`\nu=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$ and
$`\widetilde{\pi}=\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$. Assume $`\beta\leq 1`$. Fix
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}\in(0,(\tau_{0}\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}/(2L_{\beta,\eta}))`$
as a desired accuracy for approximate solving of the inner problem,
where $`\tau_{0}`$ and $`L_{\beta,\eta}`$ are defined below.

Then the following statements hold.

- (i)
  Assumption [11](#Thmassumption11 "Assumption 11 (Gradient compatibility). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  holds with
  $`C_{\mathrm{gc}}=1/(\tau_{0}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}`$ and
  $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{gc}}=0`$, where

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \tau_{0}\triangleq\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{3+\eta}{\beta(1+\eta)}}}\right),(1+1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{-1}}}\right\}\,;
  ``` |  |
- (ii)
  There exists a truncation threshold $`M_{k}>0`$ such that
  Assumption [12](#Thmassumption12 "Assumption 12 (Uniform inner-game regularity). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  holds with

  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle G`$ | $`\displaystyle=1,\quad L_{\beta,\eta}=\frac{5}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\beta(1+1/\eta)\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+3/\eta}}\right)+\beta(1+1/\eta)(4+\log|\mathcal{Y}|)\,,`$ |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle m_{\beta,\eta}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\beta(1+1/\eta)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{2}{\beta(1+1/\eta)}}}\right)\times`$ |  |
  |  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{6}{\beta(1+\eta)},2\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}{1+\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}}}\right)}}\right\}}}\right)\cdot(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}\,,`$ |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=0\,,\ \qquad\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}},\qquad\qquad\sigma^{2}_{\beta,\eta}=D_{\beta,\eta}^{2},\qquad\qquad v^{2}_{\beta,\eta}=6D_{\beta,\eta}^{2}\,,`$ |  |

  where

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  D_{\beta,\eta}^{2}=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+4\beta(1+1/\eta)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\sqrt{2}\cdot\beta(1+1/\eta)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}}}\right)+12/\eta+2\beta(1+1/\eta)\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}})}}\right)^{2}\,,
  ``` |  |

  and $`\eta`$ is chosen as solution to
  $`\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}=1`$.
- (iii)
  Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Outer update. ‣ D.1 Algorithm Description ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  outputs $`\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}})`$-optimal regularized
  policy after $`K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)`$ outer iterations,
  $`T_{k}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}})`$ inner
  iterations, using in total
  $`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}^{2})`$ samples,
  ignoring constants depending on
  $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min},\pi^{\star}_{\beta,\min},\beta,\eta`$ and
  logarithmic terms in $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$ and the confidence
  level $`\delta`$.

###### Proof.

To prove the statements, we also need to prove that for all $`k`$ it
holds
$`\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq 1`$.
We prove this statement by induction over steps $`k`$. We assume that
for all $`k^{\prime}<k`$ it holds
$`\lVert\nabla V_{k^{\prime}}(\pi_{k^{\prime}+1},\pi_{k^{\prime}+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq 1`$
for all $`k^{\prime}<k`$. This assumption allows us to use
Lemma [29](#Thmlemma29 "Lemma 29. ‣ D.4 Verification for Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
to control the minimum probability of the mixed anchor policy
$`\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$.

Next, we notice that under our improvement operator choice, for any
$`k`$ and any $`\theta\in\Theta_{\mathcal{T}_{k}}`$ it holds
$`\pi_{\theta,\min}\geq\tau_{k,0}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}`$, where
$`\tau_{k,0}`$ is defined in
Lemma [16](#Thmlemma16 "Lemma 16. ‣ C.3.2 Improvement Operator and Uniform PL ‣ C.3 Verification for the Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
with $`\nu=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$,
$`\widetilde{\pi}=\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$, and
$`\lambda=\beta(1+1/\eta)`$. In particular, we have the following
expression for $`\tau_{k,0}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tau_{k,0}=\min\Bigl\{\exp\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{\lambda}-2\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\bigr),\;(1+1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{-1}\Bigr\}\,.
``` |  |

To bound this constant away from zero uniformly over $`k`$, we need to
show that
$`\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}`$
is uniformly bounded over $`k`$. For that, we start from a definition of
geometric mixture and then apply
Lemma [27](#Thmlemma27 "Lemma 27 (Uniform regularity of PP iterates). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"):

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}=\frac{1}{1+\eta}\lVert\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\leq\frac{1/2+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{\beta(1+\eta)}\leq\frac{3}{2\beta(1+\eta)}\,.
``` |  |

Thus, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tau_{k,0}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{3+\eta}{\beta(1+\eta)}}}\right),(1+1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{-1}}}\right\}\triangleq\tau_{0}>0\,,`$ |  |

uniformly over $`k`$.

Next, we can prove the first statement of the lemma. Notice that
$`\nabla J^{\widetilde{\pi}_{k}}(\theta;\pi)=H(\pi_{\theta})\cdot\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{\theta};\pi)`$,
where
$`H(\pi_{\theta})=\mathrm{diag}(\pi_{\theta})-\pi_{\theta}\pi_{\theta}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}`$
is the parametrization Jacobian matrix. Thus, we can apply
Lemma [30](#Thmlemma30 "Lemma 30. ‣ D.4 Verification for Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
to obtain for any $`\theta\in\Theta_{\mathcal{T}_{k}}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\nabla J^{\widetilde{\pi}_{k}}(\theta;\pi)\rVert_{2}\geq\lVert\nabla J^{\widetilde{\pi}_{k}}(\theta;\pi)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\geq\pi_{\theta,\min}\cdot\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{\theta};\pi)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\geq\tau_{0}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}\cdot\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{\theta};\pi)\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}\,,
``` |  |

thus concluding the proof of the first statement with
$`C_{\mathrm{gc}}=1/(\tau_{0}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}`$ and
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{gc}}=0`$.

The second statement follows from
Proposition [5](#Thmproposition5 "Proposition 5 (Softmax parametrization satisfies Assumptions –). ‣ Parametrization. ‣ C.3 Verification for the Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
a bound on $`\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min})`$ which follows from
Lemma [29](#Thmlemma29 "Lemma 29. ‣ D.4 Verification for Softmax Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
and taking
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}`$. In
fact, a constant $`G=1`$ does not depend on $`k`$; the bound on a
smoothness coefficient follows using $`\lambda=\beta(1+1/\eta)`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle L_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{5}{2}\bigl(1+\lambda\log(1/\widetilde{\pi}_{\min})\bigr)+\lambda(4+\log|\mathcal{Y}|)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\beta(1+1/\eta)\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+\frac{1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{\eta}}}\right)+\beta(1+1/\eta)(4+\log|\mathcal{Y}|)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\beta(1+1/\eta)\log(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})+3/\eta}}\right)+\beta(1+1/\eta)(4+\log|\mathcal{Y}|)\triangleq L_{\beta,\eta}\,.`$ |  |

After that bound, we can prove the induction step to show that
$`\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq 1`$
for all $`k`$. In fact, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\mathrm{gc}}L_{\beta,\eta}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}\leq 2\cdot\frac{1}{(\tau_{0}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}}\cdot L_{\beta,\eta}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}\leq 1\,,`$ |  |

where the last inequality holds for our choice of
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$ small enough.

Next, for bound on $`m_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ we recall that under
our choice of $`\nu=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
m_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}=\lambda{\rm e}^{-2/\lambda}\cdot c_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}^{2}\,,
``` |  |

for
$`c_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}\triangleq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-2\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}},\log(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}/(1+\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}))}}\right\}}}\right)\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}`$,
where we repeat our calculations for
$`\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp}}`$
to obtain

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
c_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}\geq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{3}{\beta(1+\eta)},\log(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}/(1+\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}))}}\right\}}}\right)\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}>0\,,
``` |  |

and $`m_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}\geq m_{\beta,\eta}>0`$ uniformly over
$`k`$. Next, the bound on $`\sigma^{2}_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ and
$`v^{2}_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ again follows automatically from a
bound on $`\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min}`$, which depends on the following
quantity

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}^{2}(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}})`$ | $`\displaystyle=2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+4\lambda\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2\sqrt{2}\cdot\lambda(1+\widetilde{\pi}_{\min})}{\widetilde{\pi}_{\min}}}}\right)+2\lambda\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}})}}\right)^{2}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq 2\bigg(1+4\beta(1+\eta^{-1})\log\bigg(\frac{4\sqrt{2}\cdot\beta(1+1/\eta)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}\bigg)+\frac{12}{\eta}+2\beta(1+\eta^{-1})\log\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}\bigg)\bigg)^{2},`$ |  |

implying that the indicated bound on
$`\sigma^{2}_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ and
$`v^{2}_{\widetilde{\pi},\lambda}`$ hold uniformly over $`k`$. To
conclude the second statement of the lemma, we need to choose $`\eta>0`$
such that $`\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}=1`$. In fact, such
$`\eta`$ exists since the function
$`\eta\mapsto\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}`$ is continuous and
decreasing on $`\mathbb{R}_{>0}`$, with limits
$`\lim_{\eta\to 0}\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}=+\infty`$ and

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lim_{\eta\to+\infty}\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}=\beta\exp(-2/\beta)\cdot\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-6/\beta,2\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}{1+\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}}}}\right)}}\right\}}}\right)\cdot(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min})^{2}<1
``` |  |

under the choice $`\beta\leq 1`$.

Finally, the third statement follows from
Corollary [6](#Thmcorollary6 "Corollary 6 (Stochastic PP–SPG). ‣ D.2.4 Stochastic PP–SPG ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
noticing that our constants depend on $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$ only
logarithmically and polynomially on
$`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min},\beta,\eta`$. ∎

### D.5 Verification for Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate Parametrization

In this section, we show that along the iterations of
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Outer update. ‣ D.1 Algorithm Description ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
Assumptions [7](#Thmassumption7 "Assumption 7 (Reference-policy regularity). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")–[10](#Thmassumption10 "Assumption 10 (Compatible parametrization). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
hold uniformly over $`k`$. In particular,
Assumptions [8](#Thmassumption8 "Assumption 8 (Bounded score function). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
[9](#Thmassumption9 "Assumption 9 (Fisher Non-Degeneracy). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
and [10](#Thmassumption10 "Assumption 10 (Compatible parametrization). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
do not depend on the target policy, thus they cannot be disturbed by
iterates.

###### Lemma 31.

Assume that
$`\lVert\nabla V_{k}(\pi_{k+1},\pi_{k+1})\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}`$
for all $`k<K`$. Then
Assumption [7](#Thmassumption7 "Assumption 7 (Reference-policy regularity). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")-(i)
holds for the next step $`K`$ with

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{\widetilde{\pi}_{K}}\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x))}}\right]+\frac{2(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}})^{2}}{\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}}\,.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

First, we apply the second inequality of
Lemma [28](#Thmlemma28 "Lemma 28. ‣ D.3 Assumption Verification ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
with $`\pi=\widetilde{\pi}_{k}`$ and $`\mu=\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\pi}_{k,\min}(x)}}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log^{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x)}}}\right]+8\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}^{2}\,.
``` |  |

For the second term we use the property of geometric mixture policies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\lVert 1/(1+\eta)\log\pi_{k}+\eta/(1+\eta)\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\eta}\lVert\log\pi_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}\,.`$ |  |

Next, we apply
Lemma [27](#Thmlemma27 "Lemma 27 (Uniform regularity of PP iterates). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and achieve

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lVert\log\widetilde{\pi}_{k}-\log\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\rVert_{\mathrm{sp},\rho}\leq\frac{1}{1+\eta}\cdot\frac{1/2+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{\beta}\leq\frac{1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}}}{2\beta(1+\eta)}\,.
``` |  |

Taking a square and plugging back into the previous inequality concludes
the proof. ∎

In particular, a simple induction shows that
Assumption [7](#Thmassumption7 "Assumption 7 (Reference-policy regularity). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")-(i)
is satisfied for all $`k\in\mathbb{N}`$ simultaneously. Thus, we have
the following final corollary.

###### Corollary 8 (Convergence guarantees for Fisher compatible parameterization).

Let $`\theta\mapsto\pi_{\theta}`$ be a parametrization and
$`(\mathcal{T}_{k})_{k\geq 0}`$ a sequence of improvement operators that
satisfies
Assumption [8](#Thmassumption8 "Assumption 8 (Bounded score function). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")–[10](#Thmassumption10 "Assumption 10 (Compatible parametrization). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for all $`k\in\mathbb{N}`$, the reference policy satisfies
$`V_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}[\log^{2}(1/\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\min}(x))]<\infty`$
and let $`\beta\leq 1`$.

Then, fix $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}>0`$ as a desired accuracy for
approximate solving of the inner problem. Then the following statements
hold.

- (i)
  Assumption [11](#Thmassumption11 "Assumption 11 (Gradient compatibility). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  holds with $`C_{\mathrm{gc}}=2M_{g}^{2}/m_{F}^{2}`$ and
  $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{gc}}=2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}`$;
- (ii)
  There exists a truncation threshold $`M_{k}>0`$ such that
  Assumption [12](#Thmassumption12 "Assumption 12 (Uniform inner-game regularity). ‣ D.2.2 Inner accuracy and PP residual ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  holds with

  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle G`$ | $`\displaystyle=M_{g},\qquad m_{\beta,\eta}=\frac{\beta(1+1/\eta)m_{F}^{2}}{2M_{g}^{2}},\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PL}}=\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}\cdot m_{F}^{2}}{M_{g}^{2}}\,,`$ |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle L_{\beta,\eta}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{\eta}\cdot\sqrt{2\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}+2\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}V_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}+2(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}})^{2}}}}\right)(M_{g}^{2}+M_{h})`$ |  |
  |  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\beta(1+1/\eta)M_{g}^{2}\,,`$ |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}\,,\qquad\sigma^{2}_{\beta,\eta}=D^{2}_{\beta,\eta},\qquad v^{2}_{\beta,\eta}=6D^{2}_{\beta,\eta}\,,`$ |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle D^{2}_{\beta,\eta}`$ | $`\displaystyle\triangleq 2M_{g}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{128M_{g}^{2}[\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}(1+4V^{2}_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}})+4(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}})^{4}]}{\eta^{4}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}}}\right)\,,`$ |  |

  where
  $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}=\mathcal{O}(M_{g}^{2}/m_{F}^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}})`$
  and a PP learning rate
  $`\eta=\beta\cdot\frac{m_{F}}{\sqrt{2}\cdot M_{g}^{2}-\beta m_{F}}`$.
- (iii)
  Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Outer update. ‣ D.1 Algorithm Description ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
  outputs
  $`\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}})`$-optimal
  regularized policy after $`K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)`$ outer
  iterations,
  $`T_{k}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}^{2})`$
  inner iterations, using in total
  $`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}^{4})`$ samples,
  ignoring constants depending on
  $`M_{g},M_{h},m_{F},\beta,V_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}`$ and logarithmic
  terms in $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}`$ and
  the confidence level $`\delta`$.

###### Proof.

The statement (i) automatically follows from
Lemma [20](#Thmlemma20 "Lemma 20 (Gradient comparison). ‣ C.4.2 Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").
The statement (ii) follows from
Lemma [31](#Thmlemma31 "Lemma 31. ‣ D.5 Verification for Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
combined with
Proposition [6](#Thmproposition6 "Proposition 6 (Compatible Fisher non-degenerate parameterization satisfies Assumptions –). ‣ C.4 Verification under Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate parameterization ‣ Appendix C Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
applied for each $`k\in\mathbb{N}`$ as well as a choice of
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grad}}=\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}`$ and
manipulations to simplify the expression for $`D^{2}_{\beta,\eta}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D^{2}_{\widetilde{\pi}_{k},\lambda}(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}})`$ | $`\displaystyle=M_{g}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{8\lambda^{2}M_{g}(V_{\widetilde{\pi}_{k}}+1)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}}}}\right)^{2}\leq 2M_{g}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{(8\lambda^{2}M_{g}(V_{\widetilde{\pi}_{k}}+1))^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}}}\right)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq 2M_{g}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+64\beta^{4}(1+\eta)^{4}\frac{M_{g}^{2}(1+2V_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}}+2(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}})^{2}/(\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}))^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot\eta^{4}}}}\right)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq 2M_{g}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{128M_{g}^{2}(\beta^{2}(1+\eta)^{2}(1+4V^{2}_{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}})+4(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}})^{4})}{\eta^{4}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}}}}\right)\triangleq D^{2}_{\beta,\eta}\,,`$ |  |

where we used
Lemma [31](#Thmlemma31 "Lemma 31. ‣ D.5 Verification for Compatible Fisher Non-Degenerate Parametrization ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and an inequality $`(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}`$. Finally, we choose
$`\eta=\beta\cdot\frac{m_{F}}{\sqrt{2}\cdot M_{g}^{2}-\beta m_{F}}`$ to
guarantee that $`\beta(1+1/\eta)\cdot m_{\beta,\eta}=1`$.

For the statement (iii), we apply
Corollary [6](#Thmcorollary6 "Corollary 6 (Stochastic PP–SPG). ‣ D.2.4 Stochastic PP–SPG ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
with the constants from (ii). In particular, we choose
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PP}}=\mathcal{O}(M_{g}^{2}/m_{F}^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}}))`$
to guarantee that the final policy is
$`\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bias}})`$-VNW.
The number of outer iterations $`K`$ follows from
Corollary [6](#Thmcorollary6 "Corollary 6 (Stochastic PP–SPG). ‣ D.2.4 Stochastic PP–SPG ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and scales as $`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)`$. The number of inner
iterations $`T_{k}`$ follows from
Corollary [6](#Thmcorollary6 "Corollary 6 (Stochastic PP–SPG). ‣ D.2.4 Stochastic PP–SPG ‣ D.2 Analysis of PP–SPG ‣ Appendix D Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
and scales as
$`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}^{2})`$, where
additional $`1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$ factor comes from a bound on
$`v^{2}_{\beta,\eta}\asymp 1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}`$. Finally, the
total number of samples follows from
$`\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{k}}B_{k,t}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{in}}^{4})`$.
∎

## Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description

### E.1 Rock-Paper-Scissors

Figure 2: Optimization trajectories for the Rock–Paper–Scissors example.
Top row: exact-gradient updates. Bottom row: stochastic-gradient (SGD)
updates. Left: suboptimality versus gradient evaluations; for SGD
(bottom) we plot an exponential moving average (EMA) to smooth
single-trajectory noise. Right: trajectories in a 2D projection of the
3D parameter space ($`\Theta`$-space).

In this section, we provide an additional experiment on a very simple
3-dimensional game of Rock-Paper-Scissors. It is a context-free game
defined by the following matrix

|     |                                  |     |
|-----|----------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       {\bf P}=\begin{bmatrix}0.5&1&0\\
       0&0.5&1\\
       1&0&0.5\end{bmatrix}\,,
       ```                               |     |

a reference policy $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}=(11/18,1/3,1/18)`$, and
$`\beta=0.01`$. With this environment, we implemented in JAX ([Bradbury
et al., 2018](#bib.bib82)) an exact and stochastic versions of Online
IPO ([Calandriello et al., 2024](#bib.bib28)), EGPO ([Zhou et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31)), and $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$, using a learning rate
$`\alpha_{t}=t^{-1/2}`$ for an exact version and
$`\alpha_{t}=0.2\times t^{-1/2}`$ for a stochastic one, where $`t`$ is
an iteration number, additionally multiplied the IPO-style losses by an
effective regularization to guarantee the same scaling of updates. The
value of $`\kappa`$ is normalized to make $`10`$ total soft updates of
the target policy at the training. The results are presented on
Figure [2](#A5.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ E.1 Rock-Paper-Scissors ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").

Overall, we observe that the trajectories of $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ and
EGPO are both present more stabilized behavior compared to Online IPO,
but they are stabilized differently: two-step stabilization of EGPO
turns out to be an effective measure, especially in the beginning of
trajectory, but later the soft-anchoring of $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$
starts behaving better empirically. Additionally, we observe two facts
about the geometry of the problem: (1) suboptimality landscape exhibits
non-convex behavior, although it admits a gradient dominance geometry,
and (2) all the methods exhibits non-monotonic behavior in
suboptimality. In fact, (2) exactly prevents us to provide a
*last-iterate* convergence theory for Online IPO for small values of
$`\beta`$.

### E.2 Matrix Games

##### Experiment setup.

In our experiments, we fixed $`r=2,Y=100,\beta=0.01`$ and a reference
policy to be a uniform distribution $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y|x)=1/Y`$. The
matrices $`U`$ and $`V`$ are generated as random Gaussian matrices. To
parameterize the space of policies, we use a 3-layer MLP with a ReLU
action function and 128 hidden units, taking a flattened matrix
$`\Theta_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}`$ as input and outputting logits
over possible actions. We use the Adam optimizer ([Kingma and Ba,
2015](#bib.bib2)), and for all the baselines we perform a grid search
over the learning rate using the grid
$`\{3\times 10^{-3},10^{-3},3\times 10^{-4},10^{-4},3\times 10^{-5}\}`$.
For each batch, we sample 128 random games. For Nash Prox, we utilize
$`\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=10\times\beta`$ for all experiments. All
experiments implemented using JAX ([Bradbury et al., 2018](#bib.bib82)),
runtime for one configuration running in parallel for all 25 seeds is
less than 5 minutes for all the methods.

##### Soft vs. hard updates.

As a first additional experiment, we compare the influence of soft and
hard updates for the target network. We fixed the learning rate to
$`3\times 10^{-4}`$ and varied the update period before the update in
hard and soft senses; see
Figures [3(a)](#A5.F3.sf1 "In Figure 3 ‣ Adaptive choice of 𝜅. ‣ E.2 Matrix Games ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),[3(b)](#A5.F3.sf2 "In Figure 3 ‣ Adaptive choice of 𝜅. ‣ E.2 Matrix Games ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),[3(c)](#A5.F3.sf3 "In Figure 3 ‣ Adaptive choice of 𝜅. ‣ E.2 Matrix Games ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback")
for the results. Overall, these results show the benefits of soft
updates over hard ones in terms of suboptimality.

##### Adaptive choice of $`\kappa`$.

Next, we verify the adaptive schedule $`\kappa_{t}=1/(0.3\cdot t+1)`$,
which changes over the optimization procedure; the results are presented
in
Figure [3(d)](#A5.F3.sf4 "In Figure 3 ‣ Adaptive choice of 𝜅. ‣ E.2 Matrix Games ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback").
In particular, we observe that the adaptive schedule performs on par
with the best choice of standard soft updates; however, it is less
sensitive to the choice of optimal value of the multiplier than the
choice of optimal value of $`\kappa`$ for usual soft updates.

(a) Hard updates vs. soft updates, period 10;

(b) Hard updates vs. soft updates, period 100;

(c) Hard updates vs. soft updates, period 1000;

![Refer to caption](2505.19731v2/nash_mp_soft_vs_adaptive_schedules.png)

(d) Soft updates vs. adaptive schedule.

Figure 3: Comparison between different target policy update schedules
for $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$. Suboptimality is averaged over 25 random
seeds; shaded regions indicate one standard deviation.

### E.3 LLM Alignment

In this section, we provide implementation and details as well as
details on hyperparameter selection.

#### E.3.1 Loss implementation

We use a library TRL ([von Werra et al., 2020](#bib.bib47)) as a base
for our implementation, and we use vLLM ([Kwon et al.,
2023](#bib.bib83)) for efficient generation. Following the discussion in
Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Nash Prox: Practical Deep Learning Implementation ‣ 5 Proximal Point Method with Self-Play Policy Gradients ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"),
we use the following loss function to achieve the correct gradients
using automatic differentiation in PyTorch ([Paszke et al.,
2019](#bib.bib51))

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}}(\theta;\theta^{\mathrm{target}})`$ | $`\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ell_{\theta}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell_{\theta}(x_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})-\frac{\mathcal{P}(y_{i}\succ y^{\prime}_{i}|x_{i})-1/2}{\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}}}}\right)^{2}`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\ell_{\theta}(x,y)`$ | $`\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\beta}{\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}{\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(y|x)}}}\right)+\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{target}}}{\beta+\beta_{\mathrm{target}}}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}{\pi_{\theta^{\mathrm{target}}}(y|x)}}}\right)\,,`$ |  |

where $`\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[B]}`$ are samples from the prompt dataset,
$`\{(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i\in[B]}\}`$ are generated from a policy
$`\pi_{\theta_{t}}`$ using a temperature sampling with a temperature 1,
and $`\mathtt{SG}`$ is a stop-gradient operations.
$`\log\pi_{\theta}(y|x)`$ is computed in an auto-regressive manner as
$`\log\pi_{\theta}(y|x)=\sum_{i=1}^{|y|}\log\pi_{\theta}(y_{i}|x,y_{<i})=\sum_{i=1}^{|y|}\mathtt{logit}_{\theta}(y_{i}|x,y_{<i})-\mathtt{LogSumExp}(\mathtt{logit}_{\theta}(\cdot|x,y_{<i}))`$,
where $`\mathtt{logit}_{\theta}`$ are raw logits outputted by the model
with parameters $`\theta`$.

#### E.3.2 Experiment description

We start our experiments from the Google Gemma-3-4B¹¹ 1 Published under
Gemma license. ([Team et al., 2025](#bib.bib75)) pretrained checkpoint.

##### Supervised fine-tuning (SFT).

For SFT, we use the RLHFlow/RLHFlow-SFT-Dataset-ver2 dataset ([RLHFlow
Team, 2024c](#bib.bib42)). This dataset, structured as conversations, is
processed using a chat template following the Gemma3 format
(\<bos\>\<start_of_turn\>role\ncontent\<end_of_turn\>\n...), where the
template maps the assistant role to model. System messages are dropped
from the input, and training is performed on the train split. The
dataset samples are tokenized, with a maximum sequence length of
$`8,\!192`$ tokens. We use sample packing to efficiently train on long
sequences and pad sequences to the maximum length. Following standard
SFT practice, the loss is computed only on the model’s output tokens
(the assistant’s turns), not on the input prompts.

The model was fully fine-tuned (no LoRA PEFT adapter was used). Training
was conducted for $`2`$ epochs. Optimization was performed using a fused
version of AdamW ([Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019](#bib.bib50)) 32-bit
optimizer with a learning rate of $`1.5\times 10^{-5}`$. A cosine
learning rate schedule was applied with a warmup ratio of $`0.05`$ of
the total training steps. We used a micro batch size of $`1`$ sequence
per device and accumulated gradients over $`16`$ steps, resulting in an
effective batch size of $`16`$ sequences per device. On the 8 A100 GPUs,
we thus had an effective batch size of $`128`$. Gradient clipping was
applied with a maximum norm of $`1.0`$, and no weight decay was used.

For improved memory efficiency and speed, we enabled gradient
checkpointing and leveraged Flash Attention ([Dao, 2024](#bib.bib41)).
Training utilized BFloat16 (BF16) and TF32 precision where supported.

##### Nash Learning from Human Feedback

All subsequent NLHF experiments started from the SFT checkpoint
described above. This SFT model also served as the initial policy and
the reference policy ($`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$). During this phase, we
used LoRA adapters [Hu et al. (2022)](#bib.bib76) with rank $`r=16`$ and
$`\alpha=32`$ for all methods.

Datasets. For generating responses during NLHF training and for final
evaluation, we used a subset of prompts from the
RLHFlow/prompt-collection-v0.1 dataset ([RLHFlow Team,
2024b](#bib.bib43)). We first filtered this dataset to have only prompt
conversations of length less than $`512`$ tokens, and used 5% of prompts
as a separate validation set. Overall, we have $`\approx 80,\!200`$
train prompts and $`\approx 4,\!220`$ validation problems. For further
details on the original data mixtures within
RLHFlow/prompt-collection-v0.1 and their licenses, we refer to ([Dong et
al., 2024](#bib.bib48)).

Preference model. The pairwise preference model, used to provide
comparison signals, was a Gemma2-2B model ([Rivière et al.,
2024](#bib.bib49)). This model was trained on the
RLHFlow/pair_preference_model_dataset dataset ([RLHFlow Team,
2024a](#bib.bib44)), with its training methodology detailed in ([Liu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib40)). We employed a separate, more capable judge for
the final evaluation of model performance: a Gemma3-27-IT model prompted
to decide which completion better follows the instructions. On this
stage, we perform two episodes of judgment per prompt in two different
orders: (prompt, completion A, completion B) and (prompt, completion B,
completion A). If results were inconsistent (e.g., in both cases the
judge selected different completions depending on their order), we
consider such judgments as inconsistent and do not include them in the
win-rate computation. On average, around $`40\%`$ of the comparisons
were inconsistent.

The primary evaluation metric was side-by-side pairwise win rate, and
for hyperparameter selection in each algorithm, we used win rate against
the SFT reference policy as a proxy metric. Confidence intervals were
computed as $`99.9\%`$-confidence intervals using the first term of the
empirical Bernstein inequality for Bernoulli random variables: for an
estimate $`\hat{p}`$, we compute the intervals as
$`\sqrt{2\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})\cdot\log(2/10^{-3})\cdot 1/N}`$, where $`N`$
is a number of *consistent* judgments.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Algorithm | Learning Rate | $`\beta`$ | Algorithm-Specific Parameters |
| Online DPO | $`3\text{\times}{10}^{-5}`$ | $`1\text{\times}{10}^{-2}`$ | N/A |
| Online IPO | $`1\text{\times}{10}^{-4}`$ | $`1\text{\times}{10}^{-2}`$ | N/A |
| $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$ | $`3\text{\times}{10}^{-5}`$ | $`1\text{\times}{10}^{-3}`$ | $`\kappa_{t}=1/(0.1\times t+1)`$, $`\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=10\times\beta`$ |

Table 3: Hyperparameter settings for the evaluated algorithms. The
hyperparameters with the best performance in comparisons against the SFT
checkpoint are presented.

Training Configuration and Hyperparameter Tuning. For all NLHF
experiments, we used the AdamW optimizer ([Loshchilov and Hutter,
2019](#bib.bib50)). The learning rate schedule featured a $`0.1`$ warmup
period (as a fraction of total training steps) followed by a linear
decay. The effective global batch size was $`32`$ prompts, following the
practice of small batch sizes as indicated by [Schulman and Lab
(2025)](#bib.bib84). We used per-device micro-batch size of $`8`$
prompts, 2 GPUs A100, and $`2`$ gradient accumulation steps
($`8\text{ prompts/GPU}\times 2\text{ GPUs}\times 2\text{ grad\_accum\_steps}`$).
Training was conducted for $`1`$ epoch over the
$`N_{\mathrm{train}}=80,200`$ prompts, corresponding to approximately
$`2505`$ update steps. We employed gradient clipping with a maximum norm
of $`1.0`$.

For each algorithm, we perform a grid search over its key
hyperparameters: learning rate over
$`\mathrm{lr}\in\{3\times 10^{-4},10^{4},3\times 10^{-5}\}`$,
regularization parameter $`\beta\in\{10^{-3},10^{-2},10^{-1}\}`$, soft
update schedule of form $`\kappa_{t}=1/(ct+1)`$ for
$`c\in\{0.3,0.1,0.03\}`$. For $`\mathtt{Nash\ Prox}`$, we use always
$`\beta_{\mathrm{target}}=10\times\beta`$.

For each algorithm, we selected the best-performing hyperparameter
configurations based on the win rate against the SFT reference policy,
evaluated using the Gemma3-27B-IT judge. These selected checkpoints were
then compared side-by-side in the final evaluation using the same judge
model. The final reported results (see
Table [3](#A5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Nash Learning from Human Feedback ‣ E.3.2 Experiment description ‣ E.3 LLM Alignment ‣ Appendix E Detailed Experiment Description ‣ Proximal Point Nash Learning from Human Feedback"))
represent the performance of the best configuration found through this
process.

To manage memory and improve throughput during all NLHF training phases,
we utilized BFloat16 (BF16) mixed-precision, gradient checkpointing, and
PyTorch DDP ([Li et al., 2020](#bib.bib85)).

Computational Resources and Runtimes. All NLHF experiments were
conducted on 2 NVIDIA A100 (80GB) GPUs, with one additional GPU used to
host a pairwise reward model. A full training cycle for Nash Prox and
baselines requires approximately 11 hours per algorithm.

Generation Parameters. During response generation, both for collecting
experiences within NLHF algorithms (e.g., generating samples per prompt)
and for final evaluation on the test set, we used temperature sampling
with a temperature of $`\tau=1.0`$. The maximum generation length was
capped at 256 tokens.

##### Limitations.

We notice that using the target network increases the memory footprint
of the model, which is a limitation of our method. However, this
increase is marginal since we do not need to backpropagate through the
target model, and in the LoRA setting this memory footprint is
negligible. Additionally, it does not significantly increase the running
time of the algorithm and is straightforward to implement.
````
